id: "19347033"
title: "Breastfeeding"
---

# Breastfeeding

"Suckling" redirects here. For other uses, see [Suckling (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suckling_\(disambiguation\)).



An infant being breastfed

Video summary of article with [script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:VideoWiki/Breastfeeding)

**Breastfeeding**, also known as **nursing**, is the process whereby [breast milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_milk) is fed to an infant or toddler.[^cite_note-NIH20132-1][^cite_note-2] Infants may suckle directly from the breast, or milk may be extracted with a [breast pump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_pump) and then fed to the infant. The [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) recommends that breastfeeding begin within the first hour of a newborn's birth and continue as the baby wants.[^cite_note-WHO2014-3] Health organizations including the WHO recommend exclusively breastfeeding for six months;[^cite_note-WHO20142-4][^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5][^cite_note-Kramer_2012-6] this means that no other foods or drinks—other than [vitamin D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitamin_D) supplement—are typically given.[^cite_note-7] The WHO recommends then continuing breastfeeding with appropriate complementary foods for up to 2 years, and beyond.[^cite_note-WHO20142-4][^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5] Exclusive breastfeeding rose from 37.0% to 47.4% between 2012 and 2024.[^cite_note-WHO20142-4][^cite_note-8]

Breastfeeding has a number of benefits to both the mother and the infant, that [infant formula](65330.md) lacks.[^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5][^cite_note-IP20092-9] Increased breastfeeding to near-universal levels in low and middle income countries could prevent approximately 820,000 deaths of children under the age of five annually.[^cite_note-Victora_2016-10] Breastfeeding decreases the risk of [respiratory tract infections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_tract_infection), [ear infections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otitis_media), [ sudden infant death syndrome (SIDS)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudden_infant_death_syndrome), and [diarrhea](53951.md) for the baby, both in [developing](78449.md) and [developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country).[^cite_note-WHO20142-4][^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 13 </sup> Other benefits have been proposed to include lower risks of [asthma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asthma), [food allergies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_allergies), and [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes).[^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5][^cite_note-Victora_2016-10] Breastfeeding may also improve [cognitive development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_development) and decrease the risk of [obesity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity) in adulthood.[^cite_note-WHO20142-4][^cite_note-12]

Benefits for the mother include less [blood loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bleeding) following [delivery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childbirth), better contraction of the uterus, and a decreased risk of [postpartum depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postpartum_depression).[^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5] Breastfeeding delays the return of [menstruation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menstruation), and in very specific circumstances, [fertility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility), a phenomenon known as [lactational amenorrhea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactational_amenorrhea).[^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 83 </sup> Long-term benefits for the mother include decreased risk of [breast cancer](70547.md), [cardiovascular disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease), [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes), [metabolic syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metabolic_syndrome), and [rheumatoid arthritis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rheumatoid_arthritis).[^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5][^cite_note-Victora_2016-10][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 13 </sup> Breastfeeding is less expensive than [infant formula](65330.md),[^cite_note-APP_19972-13][^cite_note-14] but its impact on mothers' ability to earn an income is not usually factored into calculations comparing the two feeding methods.[^cite_note-15] It is also common for women to experience symptoms such as: [vaginal dryness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaginal_dryness), [De Quervain syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Quervain_syndrome), cramping, [mastitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mastitis), moderate to severe nipple pain, and a general lack of bodily autonomy. These symptoms generally peak at the start of breastfeeding but disappear or become considerably more manageable after the first few weeks.

Each feeding may last as long as 30–60 minutes, as milk supply develops and the infant learns the Suck-Swallow-Breathe pattern.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 50–51 </sup> However, as milk supply increases and the infant becomes more efficient at feeding, the duration of feeds may shorten to as little as 5-10 minutes per breast.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11][^cite_note-16]<sup>: 50–51 </sup> While newborns and young babies feed 8-12 times per 24 hours, older infants may feed less often.[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18] When direct breastfeeding is not possible, expressing or pumping to empty the breasts can help mothers avoid [plugged milk ducts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plugged_milk_duct) and [breast infection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mastitis), maintain their milk supply, resolve [engorgement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_engorgement), and provide milk to be fed to their infant at a later time.[^cite_note-NIH20132-1][^cite_note-19][^cite_note-Berens_2016-20][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 55, 63–67 </sup> Medical conditions that do not allow breastfeeding are rare.[^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5] Mothers who take certain [recreational drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recreational_drugs) should not breastfeed, however, most medications are compatible with breastfeeding.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 17 </sup> Available evidence indicates that it is unlikely that [COVID-19](63030231.md) can be transmitted through breast milk.[^cite_note-auto-21] Smoking tobacco and consuming limited amounts of alcohol or coffee are not reasons to avoid breastfeeding.[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24]

## Breastfeeding physiology



When the baby suckles their mother's breast, a hormone called [oxytocin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxytocin) compels the milk to flow from the [alveoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammary_alveolus) (lobules), through the ducts (milk canals), into the sacs (milk pools) behind the [areola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Areola), and then into the baby's mouth.



Normal histology of the breast during lactation.

Main articles: [Lactation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactation) and [Physiological changes in pregnancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiological_changes_in_pregnancy)

Breast development starts in puberty with the growth of ducts, [fat cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adipose_tissue), and connective tissue.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup> The ultimate size of the breasts is determined by the number of fat cells. The size of the breast is not related to a mother's breastfeeding capability or the volume of milk she can produce.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup> The process of milk production, termed **lactogenesis**, occurs in 3 stages. The first stage takes place during pregnancy, allowing for the development of the breast and production of colostrum, the thick, early form of milk that is low in volume but rich in nutrition.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup> The birth of the baby and the placenta trigger the onset of the second stage of milk production, triggering the milk to come in over the next several days. The third stage of milk production occurs gradually over several weeks and is characterized by a full milk supply that is regulated locally (at the breast), predominantly by the infant's demand for food. This differs from the second stage of lactogenesis, which is regulated centrally (in the brain) by hormone feedback loops that naturally occur after the placenta is delivered.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Brodribb_2018-25]

Although traditionally, lactation occurs following pregnancy, lactation may also be [induced](#Re-lactation_and_induced_lactation) with hormone therapy and nipple stimulation in the absence of pregnancy.

### Lactogenesis I and other changes in pregnancy

Changes in [pregnancy](1771587.md), starting around 16 weeks [gestational age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestational_age_\(obstetrics\)), prepare the breast for lactation. These changes, collectively known as **Lactogenesis I**, are directed by hormones produced by the placenta and the brain, namely [estrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estrogen), [progesterone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progesterone), [prolactin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prolactin), which gradually increase throughout the pregnancy, and result in the structural development of the alveolar (milk-producing) tissue and the production of [colostrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colostrum).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27] While prolactin is the predominant hormone in milk *production,* [progesterone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progesterone), which is at high levels during pregnancy, blocks the prolactin receptors in the breast, thus inhibiting milk from "coming in" during pregnancy.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Brodribb_2018-25][^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28]



Dutch woman breastfeeding

Many other physiologic changes occur under the control of progesterone and estrogen. These changes include, but are not limited to, dilation of blood vessels, increased blood flow to the uterus, increased availability of glucose (which subsequently is passed through the [placenta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Placenta) to the fetus), and increased skin pigmentation, which results in darkening of the [nipples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nipple) and [areola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Areola), formation of the [linea nigra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linea_nigra), and onset of [melasma of pregnancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melasma).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Elling_1997-29][^cite_note-Simpson-2014-30]

From about the 16th week of pregnancy, the breasts can begin to produce milk. It's not unusual for small amounts of straw-coloured fluid called [colostrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colostrum) to leak from the nipples during this relatively early stage.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33]

Breast development throughout pregnancy may result in significant [Areola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Areola) and [Areolar gland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Areolar_gland) enlargement, erectile nipples or nipple sensitivity.[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35]

### Lactogenesis II



Maternal hormone levels during pregnancy and after delivery of the placenta. Estradiol, estriol, progesterone, testosterone, and sex hormone binding globulin (SHBG) all increase throughout pregnancy and experience an abrupt drop-off after delivery of the placenta.

[^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28]

[^cite_note-Elling_1997-29]

The [third stage of labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_stage_of_labor) describes the period between the birth of the baby and the delivery of the placenta, which normally lasts less than 30 minutes.[^cite_note-Zheng_2020-36] The delivery of the placenta causes an abrupt drop off of placental hormones.[^cite_note-Zheng_2020-36][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28] This drop, specifically in progesterone, allows prolactin to work effectively at its receptors in the breast, leading to an array of changes over the next several days that allow the milk to "come in"; these changes are known collectively as Lactogenesis II.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28][^cite_note-37] [Colostrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colostrum) continues to be produced for these next few days, as Lactogenesis II occurs.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup> Milk may "come in" as late as five days after delivery; however, this process may be delayed due to a number of factors as described in the [Process](#Process) "Delay in milk 'coming in'" subsection below.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28] [Oxytocin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxytocin), which signals the [smooth muscle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smooth_muscle) of the [uterus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uterus) to contract during pregnancy, labor, birth and following delivery, is also involved in the process of breastfeeding.[^cite_note-Zheng_2020-36][^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28] Oxytocin also contracts the smooth muscle layer of band-like cells surrounding the milk ducts and alveoli to the newly produced milk through the duct system and out through the nipple.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28] This process is known as the *milk ejection reflex*, or *let-down.*[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup> Because of oxytocin's dual activity at the breast and the uterus, breastfeeding mothers may also experience uterine cramping at the time of breastfeeding, for the first several days to weeks.[^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28]

### Lactogenesis III

[Prolactin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prolactin) and [oxytocin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxytocin) are vital for establishing milk supply initially; however, once the milk supply is well established, the volume and content of the milk produced are controlled locally.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Brodribb_2018-25] Although prolactin levels are higher on average among breastfeeding mothers, prolactin levels themselves do not correlate to milk volume.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup> At this stage, production of milk is triggered by milk drainage from the breasts. The only way to maintain milk supply is to drain the breasts frequently. Infrequent or incomplete drainage of the breasts, decreases blood flow to the alveoli and signals the milk-producing cells to produce less milk.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Brodribb_2018-25][^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 72–80 </sup> [Breast pumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_pump) are often used to drain the breasts when the infant is not feeding.[^cite_note-38]

A condition called [Mastitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mastitis) sometimes occurs in this stage, resulting from incomplete milk drainage. The [Academy of Breastfeeding Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_of_Breastfeeding_Medicine?action=edit\&redlink=1) recommends against trying to "empty" the breasts, whether through pushing the baby to feed more or through over use of a breast pump, to prevent causing [milk oversupply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperlactation_syndrome).[^cite_note-\:1-39]

## Breast milk

Main article: [Breast milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_milk)



Samples of human breast milk. On the left is *foremilk*, the watery milk coming from a full breast. To the right is *hindmilk*, the creamy milk coming from a nearly empty breast.

[^cite_note-40]

The content of [breast milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_milk) should be discussed in two separate categories: the nutritional content, and the *bioactive content*, i.e., the [enzymes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzyme), [proteins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protein), [antibodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibody), and [signaling molecules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcription_factor) that assist the infant in ways outside of nutrition.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup>

### Nutritional content

At birth, the composition of breast milk corresponds in quality to the specific needs of the infant.[^cite_note-41] The pattern of intended [nutrient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutrient) content in breast milk is relatively consistent. Breastmilk is made from nutrients in the mother's [bloodstream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloodstream) and bodily stores. It has an optimal balance of fat, sugar, water, and [protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protein) that is needed for a baby's age-appropriate growth and development.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup>[^cite_note-HHS-42] That being said, a variety of factors can influence the nutritional makeup of breastmilk, including gestational age, age of infant, maternal age, maternal smoking, and nutritional needs of the infant.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup>[^cite_note-Hendrickson2012-43]

The first type of milk produced is called *[colostrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colostrum)*. The volume of colostrum produced during each feeding is appropriate for the size of the newborn's stomach and is sufficient, calorically, for feeding a newborn during the first few days of life.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] Produced during pregnancy and the first days after childbirth, colostrum is rich in protein and Vitamins A, B12 and K, which support infants' growth, brain development, vision, immune systems, red blood cells, and clotting cascade.[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-de_Vries-2018-46][^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] The breast milk also has long-chain [polyunsaturated fatty acids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyunsaturated_fatty_acids) which help with normal [retinal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retina) and [neural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural) development.[^cite_note-Colen_2014-49] The caloric content of colostrum is about 54 Calories/100mL.[^cite_note-50] The second type of milk is transitional milk, which is produced during the transition from colostrum to mature breast milk. As the breast milk matures over several weeks, the protein content decreases on average.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup> The caloric content of breastmilk is reflective of the caloric requirements of the infant, increasing steadily after 12 months.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup> The caloric content of breastmilk in the first 12 months of breastfeeding is approximated to be 58-72 Calories/100mL. Comparatively, the caloric content after 48 months is approximately 83-129 Calories/100mL.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup>

When a mother has her full milk supply and is feeding her infant, the first milk to be expressed is called the foremilk. Foremilk is typically thinner and less rich in calories. The hindmilk that follows is rich in calories and fat.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 239 </sup>

If the mother is not deficient in vitamins, breast milk normally supplies the baby's needs, except for Vitamin D. The [CDC](6811.md), [National Health Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service) (UK), [Canadian Paediatric Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Paediatric_Society), the [American Academy of Pediatrics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Academy_of_Pediatrics), and the [American Academy of Family Physicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Academy_of_Family_Physicians) all agree that breast milk alone does not provide infants with an adequate amount of Vitamin D, thus they advise parents to supplement their infants with 400 [IU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_unit) Vitamin D daily.[^cite_note-AA_Peds-2021-52][^cite_note-Casey-2010-53][^cite_note-CDC-2021c-54][^cite_note-CDC_20092-55][^cite_note-Canadian_Paediatric_Society2-56][^cite_note-NHS_Choices_Home_Page2-57] Providing this quantity of Vitamin D to breastfeeding infants has been shown to reduce rates of Vitamin D insufficiency (defined as 25-OH vitamin D < 50 nmol/L). However, there was insufficient evidence in the most recent [Cochrane Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cochrane_\(organisation\)) to determine if this quantity reduced rates of Vitamin D deficiency (defined as 25-OH vitamin D < 30 nmol/L) or [rickets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rickets).[^cite_note-58] Term infants typically do not need iron supplementation. Delaying cord clamping at birth for at least one minute improves the infants' iron status for the first year.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 50–51 </sup>[^cite_note-CDC-2021b-59] When complementary (solid) foods are introduced at about 6 months of age, parents should make sure to choose iron-rich foods to help maintain their children's iron stores.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 50–51 </sup>[^cite_note-CDC-2021b-59]

### Bioactive content

In addition to nutritional benefits, breastmilk also provides enzymes, antibodies, and other substances that support the infant's growth and development.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup> The bioactive makeup of breastmilk also changes based on the needs of the infant; for example, when an infant is recovering from an [upper respiratory infection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_respiratory_tract_infection), local signaling allows for increased passage of immune cells and proteins to aid the infant's immune system.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup>[^cite_note-60]

Produced during pregnancy and the first after childbirth, [colostrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colostrum) is easy to digest and has laxative properties that help the infant to pass early stools.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-de_Vries-2018-46] This aids in the excretion of excess [bilirubin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilirubin), which helps to prevent [jaundice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaundice).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup> Colostrum also helps to seal the infants [gastrointestinal tract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastrointestinal_tract) from foreign substances and germs, which may sensitize the baby to foods that the mother has eaten and decrease the risk of [diarrheal illness](53951.md).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup>[^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28] Although the baby has received some antibodies ([IgG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunoglobulin_G)) through the placenta, colostrum contains a substance which is new to the newborn, secretory [immunoglobulin A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunoglobulin_A) (IgA). IgA works to attack germs in the [mucous membranes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mucous_membrane) of the throat, lungs, and intestines, which are most likely to come under attack from germs.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup>[^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28][^cite_note-La_Leche_League-61] Additionally, colostrum and mature breast milk contain many [antioxidant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioxidant) and [anti-inflammatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-inflammatory) [enzymes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzyme) and [proteins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protein) that decrease the risk of gastrointestinal allergies to food, [respiratory allergies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allergic_rhinitis) to air particles like pollen, and other [atopic diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atopy), such as [asthma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asthma) and [eczema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atopic_dermatitis).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 10–14 </sup>[^cite_note-Lawrence-2016-28]

## Process

### Commencement



Checking a newborn baby's breath sounds with a [stethoscope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stethoscope)

It is recommended for mothers to initiate breastfeeding within the first hour after birth.[^cite_note-Zheng_2020-36][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-who.int-62][^cite_note-63][^cite_note-Holmes_2013-64] Uninterrupted skin-to-skin contact and breastfeeding can begin immediately after birth, and should continue for at least one hour after birth.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-Holmes_2013-64] This period of infant-mother interaction, known generally as [kangaroo care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangaroo_care), or the "golden hour" during the immediate postpartum period, assists in the mother–child bonding for both mother and baby, and is thought to encourage instinctual breastfeeding behavior in the infant.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-Breast_Crawl-65] Newborns who are immediately placed on their mother's skin have a natural instinct to latch on to the breast and start nursing, typically within one hour of birth. Success with breastfeeding in this "golden hour" increases the likelihood of successful breastfeeding at discharge.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>

Skin-to-skin mother-baby contact should still occur, even if the baby is born by [Cesarean section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cesarean_section).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-66] The baby is placed on the mother in the operating room or the recovery area. If the mother is unable to immediately hold the baby, a family member can provide skin-to-skin care until the mother is able.

#### Breast crawl

According to studies cited by [UNICEF](19698110.md), babies naturally follow a process that leads to a first breastfeed. Shortly after birth, the infant relaxes and makes small movements of the arms, shoulders, and head. If placed on the mother's abdomen, the baby gradually inches towards the breast, known as the [breast crawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_crawl)[^cite_note-Breast_Crawl-65] and begins to feed. After feeding, it is normal for a baby to remain latched to the breast while resting. This is sometimes mistaken for a lack of appetite. Without interruptions, all babies follow this process. Rushing, by picking up and moving the infant to the breast, or interrupting the process, such as removing the baby to be weighed, may complicate subsequent feeding.[^cite_note-autogenerated1-67] Activities such as weighing, measuring, bathing, needle-sticks, and eye prophylaxis wait until after the first feeding.[^cite_note-Gartner_2005-68]

#### Preterm or low-tone infants

Children who are born [preterm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preterm_birth) (before 37 weeks), children born in the early term period (37 weeks–38 weeks and 6 days), and children born with low muscular tone, such as those with [chromosomal abnormalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromosome_abnormality) like [Down syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Down_syndrome) or neurological conditions like [cerebral palsy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerebral_palsy), may have difficulty in initiating breast feeds immediately after birth.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] These [late preterm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_preterm_infant) (34 weeks –36 weeks and 6 days) and early term (37 weeks–38 weeks and 6 days) infants are at increased risk for both breastfeeding cessation and complications of insufficient milk intake (e.g., dehydration, hypoglycemia, jaundice, and excessive weight loss).[^cite_note-Boies-2016-71] They are often expected to feed like term babies, but they have less strength and stamina to feed adequately.[^cite_note-Boies-2016-71]

By convention, such children are often fed on expressed [breast milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_milk) or other supplementary feeds through tubes, [supplemental nursing systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supplemental_nursing_system), bottles, spoons, or cups until they develop a satisfactory ability to suck and swallow breast milk. Regardless of the feeding method chosen, human milk feedings, whether from the mother or a donor, are important in the brain development of premature infants, and the NICU having a standardized protocol for feeding is protective against dangerous gastrointestinal infections ([necrotizing enterocolitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Necrotizing_enterocolitis)) in these infants.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 502–545 </sup> Frequent breastfeeding or small amounts of supplementation may be needed for successful outcomes; breast pumping or [hand expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hand_expression_of_breast_milk) is often helpful in providing adequate stimulation to the mother's breasts.[^cite_note-Boies-2016-71]

Starting to breastfeed may be challenging for mothers of preterm infants, especially those born before 34 weeks, because their breasts may still be developing (in Lactogenesis I, see Breastfeeding Physiology). Additionally, mother–infant separation and the stressful environment of the NICU are also barriers to breastfeeding. Availability of a lactation specialist in the NICU can be helpful for mothers trying to establish their milk supply.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 502–545 </sup> Additionally, skin-to-skin [(Kangaroo Care)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangaroo_care) is safe and beneficial to both mother and baby.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 502–545 </sup> Kangaroo Care stabilizes newborn premature infants' vital signs, such as their heart rate, providing a naturally warm environment that helps them regulate their temperature.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 502–545 </sup> It is also beneficial to the mother, as it may improve the development of milk supply and be beneficial for mental health.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>

### Timing

Newborn babies usually breastfeed 8 to 12 times every 24 hours, and they typically express hunger cues every one to three hours for the first two to four weeks of their lives.[^cite_note-Zheng_2020-36][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 50–51 </sup>[^cite_note-cpmc-72] A [newborn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant) has a small stomach capacity, approximately 20 mL.[^cite_note-73] The amount of breast milk that is produced is timed to meet the infant's needs in that the first milk, colostrum, is concentrated but produced in only very small amounts, gradually increasing in volume to meet the expanding size of the infant's stomach capacity.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-La_Leche_League-61] [Cluster feeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cluster_feeding) may occur for several months, whereby infants will feed very frequently (every 30 minutes to 1 hour), usually associated with [developmental milestones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_development_stages).[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75]

Many newborns typically feed for 10 to 15 minutes on each breast; however, feeds may last up to 45 minutes, depending on the infant's alertness and efficiency.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 50–51 </sup>[^cite_note-NIH2014How-76]

Nutritive sucking follows a slow, rhythmic pattern, with one to two sucks per swallow. Non-nutritive sucking is a faster-paced sucking pattern with few swallows. This swallow pattern is often observed at the beginning or the end of a feed. At the beginning of the feed, this pattern triggers milk letdown, while at the end of the feed, this may signal that the infant is tired or becoming relaxed with a slower milk velocity.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>

### Duration and exclusivity

Numerous health organizations, including, but not limited to, the CDC, WHO, National Health Service, Canadian Pediatric Society, American Academy of Pediatrics, and American Academy of Family Physicians, recommend breastfeeding exclusively for six months following birth, unless medically contraindicated.[^cite_note-WHO20142-4][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 15–17 </sup>[^cite_note-USCDC-77][^cite_note-Health_Canada2-78][^cite_note-Australian_Government_Department_of_Health-79][^cite_note-UKNHS-80][^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5][^cite_note-CDC2011-81][^cite_note-Promotion_of_Breastfeeding_in_Europe-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84][^cite_note-AAFP_position_paper-85][^cite_note-ACOG_756-86] Exclusive breastfeeding is defined as "an infant's consumption of human milk with no supplementation of any type (no water, no juice, no nonhuman milk and no foods) except for vitamins, minerals and medications."[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 15–17 </sup>[^cite_note-Gartner_2005-68] Supplementation with human donor breastmilk may be indicated in some specific cases, as discussed below.[^cite_note-Abrams_2017-87] After solids are introduced at around six months of age, continued breastfeeding is recommended. The American Academy of Pediatrics recommends that babies be breastfed at least until 12 months, or longer if both the mother and child wish.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 15–17 </sup>[^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5] The World Health Organization's guidelines recommend "continue\[d] frequent, on-demand breastfeeding until two years of age or beyond.[^cite_note-who.int-62][^cite_note-WHO2003-88] The American Academy of Pediatrics policy statement also supports continued breastfeeding to 2 years and older, depending on the mother and child.[^cite_note-\:2-89]

[Extended breastfeeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extended_breastfeeding) means breastfeeding after the age of 12 or 24 months, depending on the source. In Western countries such as the [United States](3434750.md), [Canada](5042916.md), and [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain), extended breastfeeding is relatively uncommon and can provoke criticism.[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-Mayo_Clinic-91]

Globally exclusive breastfeeding rose from 37.0% to 47.4% between 2012 and 2024;.[^cite_note-WHO20142-4] In the United States, 35% of babies born in 2018 were breastfed for 12 months, and 25.8% were exclusive breastfed for 6 months, as recommended by the American Academy of Pediatrics, though rates vary by race and ethnicity.[^cite_note-\:2-89] In [India](14533.md), mothers commonly breastfeed for 2 to 3 years.[^cite_note-Stein_2004-92]

#### Supplementation

Supplementation is defined as the use of additional milk or fluid products to feed an infant, in addition to breastmilk, during the first 6 months of life.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>[^cite_note-Kellams_2017-93] The Academy of Breastfeeding Medicine recommends only supplementing when medically indicated, as opposed to mixing use of formula and breastmilk for reasons that are not necessarily medical indications.[^cite_note-Kellams_2017-93] Some medical indications for supplementation include low blood sugar, dehydration, excessive weight loss or poor gain, and jaundice in the infant; true low milk supply; severe nipple pain unrelieved by interventions; and medical contraindications to breastfeeding, as described below.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>[^cite_note-Kellams_2017-93] Supplements can be delivered at the breast through a [supplemental nursing system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supplemental_nursing_system) to stimulate the production of the mother's own milk and to preserve the breastfeeding relationship.[^cite_note-94] Some parents may desire to supplement proactively if early signs of insufficient intake, such as decreased urination, dry mucous membranes, or persistent signs of hunger, are noticed. If these signs are noticed, it is important to have the mother-infant dyad evaluated by a breastfeeding specialist or pediatrician to determine the true cause of the symptoms and determine the need for supplementation.[^cite_note-Kellams_2017-93] Often, these symptoms are caused by poor milk transfer at the breast and can be solved with adjustments to the latch. Occasionally, they may be caused by other processes, unrelated to breastfeeding, so evaluation is necessary.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 72–73 </sup> Supplementation with formula is associated with decreased rates of exclusive breastfeeding at 6 months, and overall decreased length of breastfeeding.[^cite_note-Kellams_2017-93]

In terms of *what* to supplement with, the first choice is always the mother's own breastmilk, save any medical contraindications to its use.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>[^cite_note-Kellams_2017-93] The second best option for supplementation is [pasteurized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasteurization) human [donor milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_milk_bank).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>[^cite_note-Kellams_2017-93] Finally, specific formulas may be used for supplementation if maternal or donor breastmilk is not an option.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>[^cite_note-Kellams_2017-93] One situation where this may be the case is in cases of infant metabolic diseases, such as [galactosemia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactosemia).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 15–17 </sup>[^cite_note-Kellams_2017-93] The Academy of Breastfeeding Medicine recommends that supplementation only be used when medically indicated and when overseen by a medical professional, such as a pediatrician or family physician, and after consultation with an IBCLC.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup> Without sufficient breast stimulation, supplementation can reduce the mother's milk production, so pumping would be indicated in these cases if continued breastfeeding is desired.

Indications for use of [donor breastmilk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_milk_bank) are very closely outlined by the [American Academy of Pediatrics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Academy_of_Pediatrics) (AAP). Due to low availability and high cost of donor breastmilk, the AAP recommends prioritizing the use of the milk for infants born with a weight of less than 1500g (approximately 3lb 5oz), as it is helpful in decreasing rates of the severe intestinal infection, [necrotizing enterocolitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Necrotizing_enterocolitis), in this population.[^cite_note-Abrams_2017-87]

### Position



A mother in Russia cradling an infant while breastfeeding

Effective positioning and technique for latching on are necessary to prevent nipple soreness and allow the baby to obtain enough milk.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 50–51 </sup>[^cite_note-95]

Babies can successfully latch on to the breast from multiple positions. Each baby may prefer a particular position. The "football" hold places the baby's legs next to the mother's side with the baby facing the mother. This hold can be helpful for breastfeeding mothers recovering from a caesarean section (C-section). Using the "cradle" or "cross-body" hold, the mother supports the baby's head in the crook of her arm. The "cross-over" hold is similar to the cradle hold, except that the mother supports the baby's head with the opposite hand. The mother may choose a reclining position on her back or side with the baby lying next to her.[^cite_note-96] To help with breastfeeding, some people use a [nursing pillow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursing_pillow).[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98]

No matter the position the mother-infant dyad finds most comfortable, there are a few components of every position that will help facilitate a successful [latch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latch_\(breastfeeding\)). One key component is maternal comfort. The mother should be comfortable while breastfeeding, and should have her back, feet, and arms supported with pillows as necessary. Additionally, when starting the latch process, the infant should be aligned with their abdomen facing their mother, which can be remembered as "tummy-to-mummy," and with their hips, shoulders, and head aligned. This alignment helps to facilitate proper, efficient swallowing mechanics.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>

### Latching

Main article: [Latch (breastfeeding)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latch_\(breastfeeding\))

Latching refers to how the baby uses their lips, cheek, and tongue to attach to the nipple and breast.

#### Making use of anatomy and reflexes

The process of latching a newborn onto the breast

[^cite_note-Newman-99]

&#x20;Sebaceous glands called [Glands of Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glands_of_Montgomery) located in the areola secrete an oily fluid that lubricates and protects the nipple during latching. The visible portions of the glands can be seen on the skin's surface as small, round bumps.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>

The [rooting reflex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rooting_reflex) is the baby's natural tendency to turn towards the breast with the mouth open wide.[^cite_note-100] When preparing to latch, mothers should make use of this reflex by gently stroking the baby's [philtrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philtrum), the area between the upper lip and the nose, with their nipple to induce the baby to open their mouth with a wide gape.[^cite_note-Newman-99] One way to help the infant achieve a deep latch is to compress the breast tissue into a "U" or "hamburger shape," so that the infant can fit the breast tissue into their mouth. This is done by the mother placing her thumb and fingers in line with the infant's nose and mouth, respectively, and using this grip to compress the breast tissue.[^cite_note-Newman-99]

#### Bringing the infant in to latch



Illustration noting the lower lip of the infant during latching

If the newborn seems to need help in latching on, the mother should focus on helping the infant by bringing their chin to the breast first. This facilitates a deep, asymmetric latch, and also helps the infant [extend their neck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatomical_terms_of_motion) and tilt their forehead back to maintain this deep latch and ease the swallowing process.[^cite_note-101]

#### Signs of a good, deep latch



Example and indicators of a good latch

[^cite_note-ibconline.ca-102]

[^cite_note-Simpson-2014-30]

In a good latch, a large amount of the areola, in addition to the nipple, is in the baby's mouth. The amount of areola visible on either side of the infant's mouth should be **asymmetric**, meaning most of the "bottom" of the areola should be in the infant's mouth, and much more of the "top" of the areola should be visible. This position helps point the nipple toward the roof of the infant's mouth, helping the infant recruit more milk.[^cite_note-ibconline.ca-102] The baby's lips should be flanged out.[^cite_note-NHS_2016-103][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceLawrence2015249-104] The neck should be [extended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatomical_terms_of_motion) to facilitate swallowing, and as such, the chin will be close to the breast, and the forehead and nose should be far from the breast.[^cite_note-ibconline.ca-102] Another sign of a good latch is the contour of the infant's cheeks; the cheeks should be rounded all the way to the edge of the mouth, rather than dimpled or creased at the edge of the mouth.[^cite_note-105] This is a good indicator of effective suck mechanics. Additionally, to achieve a deep latch, the infant's mouth must be open wide, preferably wider than 140 degrees.[^cite_note-Simpson-2014-30]

#### Signs of a poor, shallow latch



Example and indicators of a poor, shallow latch

[^cite_note-ibconline.ca-102]

In a poor, shallow latch, the infant may latch close to, or at, the nipple, which can cause the mother pain.[^cite_note-LLL_GB-2016-106] While the infant is at the breast, the first indicators of a shallow latch are having the [areola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Areola) be largely visible outside the infant's mouth and a narrow infant mouth angle.[^cite_note-ibconline.ca-102] Additional signs result from poor positioning when the infant comes toward the breast to latch. If the infant leads with their brow or forehead, they are likely to flex their neck; this latching mechanism causes the nipple to point down and then hit the hard palate during sucking.[^cite_note-LLL_GB-2016-106] From an external view, this manifests as the nose and forehead being close to the breast and the chin far from the breast.[^cite_note-ibconline.ca-102] This neck flexion also obstructs the normal swallowing mechanism, preventing the infant from drinking efficiently.[^cite_note-ibconline.ca-102] In addition to not being able to swallow properly, this shallow latch prevents the infant from adequately compressing the glandular tissue behind the nipple and stimulating milk flow; thus, they may begin to apply more suction, which manifests externally as cheek dimpling, or sucking their cheeks in.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-ibconline.ca-102]

### Let-down reflex

See also: [Lactation § Milk ejection reflex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactation#Milk_ejection_reflex)

When the baby suckles, muscles in the breast squeeze milk towards the nipples. This is called the let-down reflex. Some women report they do not experience anything, while others report a tingling sensation, which is sometimes described as quite strong. The baby may be seen to respond to the start of milk flow by changing from quick sucks to deep rhythmic swallows. Sometimes the let-down is so strong that the baby splutters and coughs, and the mother may need to remove the baby from her breast for a short time until the flow becomes less forceful. Milk may also let down unexpectedly when a mother hears her baby cry or even only thinks about the baby. Nursing pads may be made or purchased to absorb unexpected milk flows.[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108]

### Problems with breastfeeding

Main article: [Breastfeeding difficulties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breastfeeding_difficulties)

#### Inverted nipples

Main article: [Inverted nipple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverted_nipple)

Infants of mothers with inverted nipples can still achieve a good latch with perhaps a little extra effort. For some women, the nipple may easily become erect when stimulated. Other women may require modified breastfeeding techniques, and some may need extra devices, such as nipple shells, modified syringes, or breast pumps to expose the nipple.[^cite_note-LLLI-2021b-109][^cite_note-Cheung_2017-110] La Leche League and Toronto Public Health offer several techniques to use during pregnancy or even in the early days following birth that may help to bring a flat or inverted nipple out.[^cite_note-LLLI-2021b-109][^cite_note-Cheung_2017-110]

#### Use of pacifiers

The World Health Organization's Ten Steps to Successful Breastfeeding recommends total avoidance of pacifiers for breastfeeding infants. In 2016, a large review of studies reported that the use of a pacifier beginning at birth or after lactation was established did not significantly affect the duration of exclusive and partial breastfeeding up to four months of age. The CDC, however, currently (2022) reports that early use of pacifiers can have a negative outcome on the success of breastfeeding, and they suggest that it should be delayed until breastfeeding is firmly established.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-Marmet_2000-112][^cite_note-JaafarHo2016-113]

#### Ankyloglossia

Main article: [Ankyloglossia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankyloglossia)



Anterior tongue-tie on a four-year-old

[^cite_note-114]

Ankyloglossia, also called "tongue-tie" may cause shallow latch, poor milk transfer, and other problems with breastfeeding.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup> There are two types of tongue-ties: an anterior tongue-tie occurs when a band of tissue, known as the [frenulum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frenulum_of_tongue), attaches the tongue to the base of the mouth, restricting the tongue's vertical movement and preventing the infant from pressing the breast and nipple into the [soft palate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_palate);[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup> a posterior tongue-tie is a band of tissue that can only be *felt* on exam, and tends to impact breastfeeding less severely than its anterior counterpart.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup> If it is determined that the inability to latch on properly is related to ankyloglossia, a simple surgical procedure to clip the frenulum can correct the condition.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-whgov-116][^cite_note-whgov2-117][^cite_note-118] The Academy of Breastfeeding Medicine and the Australian Dental Association have raised concern over the growing trend of oral tie surgeries, due to evidence for benefit being low-quality, inconsistent, or unsupported.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122]

#### Engorgement

Main article: [Breast engorgement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_engorgement)



Pre-pregnant human breasts



Lactating human breasts showing evidence of engorgement

Engorgement is the swelling and stretching of the breast tissue due to the accumulation of fluid in the tissue surrounding and supporting the milk-producing cells and ducts.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 55 </sup>[^cite_note-Berens_2016-20] Engorgement most frequently occurs as milk "comes in" and during the [weaning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaning) process.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 55 </sup>[^cite_note-123] As milk is coming in, several processes occur. At the end of pregnancy, the blood vessels that supply the breast dilate, allowing for leaking into the tissue or [interstitial space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstitial_fluid).[^cite_note-Berens_2016-20] Additionally, the birth of an infant is followed by massive fluid shifts to both offload excess fluid, which had been used to supply oxygen and nutrients to the fetus through the placenta, which is no longer needed, and supply additional fluid to the breasts to start the process of making milk. These fluid shifts often result in some of this excess fluid leaking into the breast tissue.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 55 </sup> Finally, milk "coming in" can create an uncomfortably full feeling, which, combined with the aforementioned fluid accumulation in the breast tissue, can cause severe pain.[^cite_note-Berens_2016-20][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 55 </sup> If breastfeeding is suddenly stopped, the breasts are likely to become engorged. Pumping small amounts to relieve discomfort helps to train the breasts to produce less milk gradually. There is presently no safe medication to prevent engorgement, but cold compresses and [ibuprofen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibuprofen) may help to relieve pain and swelling. Pain should go away with emptying of the breasts. If symptoms persist and comfort measures are not helpful, a [blocked milk duct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blocked_milk_duct) or infection may be present, and the affected person should seek medical intervention.[^cite_note-WebMed,_LLC-124]

#### Nipple pain

Main article: [Nipple pain in breastfeeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nipple_pain_in_breastfeeding)

Although very common, nipple pain and nipple trauma (cracking, open sores) should not be normalized, as these are often signs of a shallow latch or other underlying problem that can be evaluated and fixed.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup> In addition to shallow latch, other causes of nipple pain include, but are not limited to, skin infection or [inflammation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dermatitis), [blood vessel spasm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasospasm) or the equivalent of [Raynaud Syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raynaud_syndrome) in the breast, [mastitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mastitis), plugged ducts, and [nipple blebs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nipple_bleb).[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 572–593 </sup>[^cite_note-Berens_2016b-125] Pain caused by a problem deep in the breast may also present with nipple pain due to the paths of nerves in the breast.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 572–593 </sup> In addition to the serious nature of many of these causes, nipple pain is a common reason for a mother stopping breastfeeding, so mothers experiencing nipple pain must be evaluated.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 572–593 </sup>[^cite_note-Berens_2016b-125]

#### Delay in milk "coming in"

Main article: [Delayed onset of lactation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delayed_onset_of_lactation)

While milk normally "comes in" by 3 days after birth, there are several reasons this may be delayed. Risk factors for this delay include [maternal diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes_and_pregnancy), stressful delivery, [retained placenta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retained_placenta), prolonged labor and birth by [C-section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesarean_section).[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 84 </sup>[^cite_note-Berens_2016-20][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup> Mothers experiencing a delay in their milk coming in should consult with a lactation specialist and their pediatrician, as they may need to supplement with donor milk or formula to help the infant gain weight and pump to encourage milk to come in sooner and in greater volume.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>

#### Low milk supply

Main article: [Low milk supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_milk_supply)

Breast milk supply increases in response to the baby's demand for milk and decreases when milk is allowed to remain in the breasts.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 18–21 </sup>[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 27–34 </sup>[^cite_note-Brodribb_2018-25][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 72–80 </sup>[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceLawrence201567-126] When considering a possibly low milk supply, it is important to consider the difference between "perceived low milk supply" and "true low milk supply". Perceived low milk supply occurs when mothers, for a variety of reasons, believe that they are not making enough milk to feed their infant.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 72–80 </sup> These reasons may include fussiness, [colic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_colic), preference for the bottle as opposed to the breast, long nursing duration, decreased sensation of breast fullness, and even decreased frequency of infant stools.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 72–80 </sup> However, in these cases, it is important to reassure the parent that infant weight gain is absolute proof of adequate milk intake.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 49 </sup> Thus, if the infant is breastfeeding exclusively and is gaining weight appropriately, then the parent can be reassured that they are producing enough milk.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 49 </sup>

True low milk supply can be either *primary* (caused by medical conditions or anatomical issues in the mother), *secondary* (caused by not thoroughly and regularly removing milk from the breasts), or both. Primary causes may manifest before or during pregnancy, during labor, and even after birth.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 72–73 </sup> Secondary causes are far more common than primary ones. One study found that 15% of healthy first-time mothers had low milk supply 2–3 weeks after birth, with secondary causes accounting for at least two-thirds of those cases.[^cite_note-neifert-127]

Poor milk intake is signaled by poor infant weight gain, signs of dehydration, and hypoglycemia.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 49 </sup>[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup> Poor milk intake can be caused by poor milk transfer by the infant or by true low milk supply by the mother.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 52–54 </sup> When the milk "comes in" appropriately, but is followed by decreased [milk supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_milk_supply), this is most often caused by allowing milk to remain in the breasts for long periods of time, or insufficiently draining the breasts during feeds.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 72–80 </sup> If the baby is [latching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latch_\(breastfeeding\)) and swallowing well (signs of good milk transfer), but is not gaining weight as expected or is showing signs of dehydration, low milk supply in the mother can be suspected, and a lactation specialist should be consulted.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 52–54 </sup>[^cite_note-neifert-127]

#### Newborn jaundice

Main article: [Neonatal jaundice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neonatal_jaundice)

More than 80% of newborns develop [jaundice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaundice) within several days of birth.[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] [Jaundice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neonatal_jaundice), or yellowing of the skin and eyes, occurs when [bilirubin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilirubin), a byproduct of the breakdown/recycling of red blood cells, builds up in the newborn's bloodstream faster than the liver can break it down and excrete it through the baby's urine and stool. By continuing to breastfeed frequently (start at 8-12 times per day), the infant's body can usually rid itself of the bilirubin excess by encouraging more urine and stool production.[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] However, in some cases, the infant may need additional treatments, such as [UV light therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_therapy) or additional feedings (see Supplementation) to keep the condition from progressing into more severe problems.[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44][^cite_note-cdc.gov-128]

There are two types of newborn jaundice related to breastfeeding.

Breastfeeding jaundice is quite common and may occur in the first week of life in conjunction with ongoing weight loss.[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] The cause is thought to be low caloric intake. Formula-fed infants tend to lose less weight after birth compared to breastfed infants, supporting the hypothesis that breastfeeding jaundice is related to caloric intake rather than volume intake.[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] Individual risk factors, such as breastfeeding, are not *predictive* of developing severe jaundice: Breastfeeding is a risk factor for severely high levels of bilirubin, but the risk factor is very common, and the risk of severely high bilirubin remains small.[^cite_note-AA_Peds-2004-129]

Breast milk jaundice is jaundice that persists despite appropriate weight gain.[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] This type of jaundice may start as breastfeeding jaundice and persist, or may not appear until after the baby has begun to gain weight, typically around 4–5 days old.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 34–47 </sup>[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] It often persists beyond the second and third weeks of life.[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] There is no single cause of breast milk jaundice; rather, the causes are multifactorial and frequently debated in the literature.[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] The causes of breast milk jaundice include variations in [bilirubin metabolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilirubin_metabolism), [genetic variations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_variation), and variations in breastmilk, including the [harmless and helpful germs found naturally on the surface of the skin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_microbiome) and in the breastmilk.[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44] Breast milk jaundice is usually not a reason to stop nursing.[^cite_note-cdc.gov-128] It is important to consult with a physician to determine when it may be necessary to test for other causes of jaundice that may require additional treatment, such as enzyme deficiencies or problems with the [red blood cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_blood_cell) (i.e., [elliptocytosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hereditary_elliptocytosis), [spherocytosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hereditary_spherocytosis), [hemolysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemolysis), [glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase deficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glucose-6-phosphate_dehydrogenase_deficiency)).[^cite_note-Flaherman_2017-44]

### Weaning

Weaning is the process of replacing breast milk with other foods; the infant is fully weaned after the replacement is complete.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 112–116 </sup>[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11] Psychological factors affect the weaning process for both mother and infant, as issues of closeness and separation are very prominent.[^cite_note-130] Unless a medical emergency necessitates abruptly stopping breastfeeding, it is best to gradually increase the period between feedings or eliminate feedings to allow the breasts to adjust to the decreased demands without becoming [engorged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_engorgement).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11] Studies show that a large number of women discontinue breastfeeding early due to lack of working place support for breastfeeding mothers.[^cite_note-today.com-131] La Leche League advises parents to shift their children's focus at bedtime away from breastfeeding, as it is often the most difficult feeding for them to let go.[^cite_note-La_Leche_League_International-132]

If weaning starts at 12 months or later, it is not necessary to switch to infant formula or "toddler formula" as is sold commercially. At 12 months, it is recommended that the baby be switched to whole cow's milk. Reduced-fat or skim milk generally is not appropriate before age 2 because it lacks sufficient fat or calories to promote early brain development.[^cite_note-133]

If the mother was experiencing [lactational amenorrhea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactational_amenorrhea_method), periods will begin to return with weaning, along with restored fertility.[^cite_note-134]

### Extended breastfeeding

Main article: [Extended breastfeeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extended_breastfeeding)

Extended breastfeeding usually means breastfeeding beyond the age of 12 to 24 months, depending on the culture. The American Academy of Family Physicians states that "health outcomes for mothers and babies are best when breastfeeding continues for at least two years.[^cite_note-135] The American Academy of Pediatrics recommends that mothers nurse for the first 12 months and "thereafter for as long as mother and baby desire." The World Health Organization recommends breastfeeding up to age 2 "or beyond."[^cite_note-today.com-131]

Breast milk is known to contain [lactoferrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactoferrin) (Lf), which protects the infant from infection caused by a wide range of [pathogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathogens). The amount of Lf in breast milk is lactation-stage related. One study evaluated Lf concentration in prolonged lactation from the first to the 48th month postpartum. It was found to be at the highest level in colostrum, dropped to the lowest level during 1 – 12 months of lactation, and then increased significantly during the 13–24 months of lactation, close to the Lf concentration in colostrum. At over 24 months, the level dropped, though not significantly.[^cite_note-136]

### Professional breastfeeding support

[Lactation consultants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactation_consultants) are trained to assist mothers in preventing and solving breastfeeding difficulties such as sore nipples and low milk supply. They commonly work in hospitals, physician or midwife practices, public health programs, and private practice. Lactation consultants earn their credential, International Board Certified Lactation Consultant (IBCLC), through the International Board of Lactation Consultant Examiners.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-ussg-139][^cite_note-140]

Breastfeeding support from a lactation consultant is associated with higher rates of any breastfeeding at 6 months but not at 1 month or 3 months post pregnancy based on a meta-analysis of studies conducted in the US and Canada.[^cite_note-\:0-141] Peer support for breastfeeding is associated with higher rates of any breastfeeding at 1 month and 3 to 6 months and of exclusive breastfeeding at 1 month, but it is unrelated to breastfeeding outcomes past 6 months post-pregnancy.[^cite_note-\:0-141]

## Maternal diet and substance use

Main article: [Breastfeeding difficulties § Substance use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breastfeeding_difficulties#Substance_use)

#### Diet

In addition to increased fluid intake, breastfeeding women need additional calories to support milk production. This is estimated to be upwards of 300 calories per day.[^cite_note-\:4-142][^cite_note-143]

Mercury in fish is an important consideration for breastfeeding women. Though fish can be a good protein source, fish also contain [mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_in_fish), which can be harmful. The concentration of mercury varies by fish species.[^cite_note-\:4-142]

##### Alcohol

See also: [Alcohol and pregnancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_and_pregnancy)

Moderate [alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_\(drug\)) consumption, one standard drink per day, by breastfeeding mothers is not known to significantly affect infants; however, the American Academy of Pediatrics strongly discourages more than two standard drinks per day.[^cite_note-\:2-89] Even one or two drinks, including beer, may reduce milk intake by 20 to 23%, leading to increased agitation and poor sleep patterns. Regular heavy drinking (more than two drinks daily) can shorten breastfeeding duration and cause issues in infants, such as excessive sedation, fluid retention, and hormonal imbalances. Additionally, higher alcohol consumption may negatively impact children's [academic achievement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_achievement).[^cite_note-144]

When breastfeeding, alcohol may be consumed in moderation and does not require "Pumping-and-Dumping" (pumping and discarding breastmilk).[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup>[^cite_note-145] Alcohol crosses from the blood to the breastmilk by [diffusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffusion). Thus, the concentration of alcohol in the breastmilk is approximately equal to the concentration in the maternal bloodstream at any given time.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup>[^cite_note-Haastrup_2014-146] As the mother's liver processes the alcohol, more and more alcohol is pulled out of the breastmilk and back into the bloodstream.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup> Thus, it is suggested to wait 2 hours after drinking before nursing or pumping.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup>[^cite_note-AA_Peds-2017-147] In the case of infrequent binge drinking, it has been shown that infants consume through breastmilk only a fraction of the alcohol their mothers have ingested. While a minute, clinically insignificant amount of alcohol may be absorbed into the infant's bloodstream, it is unlikely that this amount would cause any noticeable cognitive or neuromotor effects.[^cite_note-Haastrup_2014-146]

##### Recreational drugs

The data on the use of [marijuana](1481886.md) during breastfeeding is limited; however, in part due to our lack of knowledge in this area, the CDC recommends against using marijuana or marijuana-containing products, including [CBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBD_Oil), during breastfeeding.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-NLM-2006-149] The main active ingredient in marijuana, [tetrahydrocannabinol (THC)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetrahydrocannabinol), can be found in breastmilk anywhere from six days to more than six weeks after marijuana use.[^cite_note-NLM-2006-149] There is limited data on the long term effects of this exposure on the infant, however some studies have voiced concern regarding delayed motor development in infants exposed to THC.[^cite_note-NLM-2006-149]

##### Tobacco

Mothers who smoke or use other [tobacco](30942.md) products can breastfeed their infants, according to [La Leche League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Leche_League), the [CDC](6811.md), and the [Royal Women's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Women's_Hospital) (Australia).[^cite_note-CDC-2021d-150][^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152] However, maternal tobacco use may decrease milk supply.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup> Additionally, tobacco smoking, regardless of feeding method, increases risk of SIDS and respiratory illnesses.[^cite_note-CDC-2021d-150][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup> Thus, attempting to decrease tobacco use or even cease helps to minimize tobacco exposure to infants and maximize the benefits of breastfeeding.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup> [Tobacco cessation products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoking_cessation), such as [nicotine patches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicotine_patch) and other medications, like [bupropion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bupropion), are able to be used by breastfeeding mothers.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup>

##### Other recreational drugs

Mothers utilizing recreational drugs, such as cocaine, methamphetamines, PCP, and heroin, should not breastfeed.[^cite_note-CDC-2021e-153][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 17 </sup>

#### Medication use

Most medications are compatible with continued breastfeeding.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 17 </sup>[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup> Many medicines pass into breastmilk in small amounts; however, very few medications actually reach the infant and are absorbed in a way that would actually impact the infant.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup> Several characteristics of medications, including size and pH of the medication molecule and how well the medication is absorbed in the GI tract, influence how much of a medication may reach, and may ultimately be absorbed, by the infant.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup> In addition to the effects on the infant, many medications are known to significantly suppress milk production, including [pseudoephedrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoephedrine), [diuretics](40496970.md), and [contraceptives](18978770.md) that contain [estrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estrogen).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceLawrence2015390–392-154][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup>

There are several resources to assist medical professionals in determining which medications are safe for pregnancy and breastfeeding. While patients can use these resources, they are targeted toward medical professionals. Patients should be encouraged to consult a lactation specialist or a medical provider trained in breastfeeding medicine if any concerns arise. Two helpful resources are listed below.

1. LactMed @ NIH (Drugs and Lactation Database (LactMed))

   [^cite_note-155]

2. InfantRisk App (InfantRisk Center at Texas Tech University Health Sciences Center)

   [^cite_note-156]

##### Pumping-and-dumping

"Pumping-and-dumping" is the concept of expressing breastmilk and discarding it due to a medication or substance "tainting" the breastmilk.[^cite_note-NW_Med-2021-157] It was once believed that drinking alcohol or taking any medications, even medicines like ibuprofen, required pumping-and-dumping.[^cite_note-NW_Med-2021-157] However, this is no longer the case. Pumping-and-dumping, or stopping breastfeeding altogether, is only required in very rare circumstances, such as with radioactive medications or chemotherapy.[^cite_note-LLLI-2018-158][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 105–109 </sup>

If a parent is concerned with a possible milk contaminant, they can express and save the breastmilk until they can consult with a lactation specialist or another medical professional trained in breastfeeding medicine.

## Contraindications to breastfeeding

### Maternal contraindications

Medical conditions that do not allow breastfeeding are fairly rare.[^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5] Infants that are otherwise healthy uniformly benefit from breastfeeding,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceLawrence2015Chapter_7._Facilitating_an_Informed_Decision_About_Breastfeeding_pp._215–232-159] however, extra precautions should be taken or breastfeeding avoided in circumstances including certain infectious diseases and medical conditions.

#### Maternal infections

##### [HIV](14170.md)

A breastfeeding child can become infected with HIV. Factors such as the mother's [viral load](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_load) complicate breastfeeding recommendations for HIV-positive mothers. The [World Health Organization](33583.md) highlights the possibility of breastfeeding in mothers on [anti-viral therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV_treatment) and with undetectable [viral loads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_load_monitoring_for_HIV), especially in areas where access to clean water is poor and where death from infectious diseases is common, citing low transmission rates when the mother is on anti-viral therapy.[^cite_note-WHO20142-4][^cite_note-WHO-2014-160][^cite_note-Aus_DOH-2017-161] They also recommend that national authorities in each country decide which infant feeding practice should be promoted by their maternal and child health services to avoid transmitting HIV from mother to child.[^cite_note-Mead-162] However, the [CDC](6811.md) continues to recommend against HIV-positive mothers breastfeeding in the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-Aus_DOH-2017-161][^cite_note-163] Infant formula should only be given if this can be safely done.[^cite_note-WHO20142-4]

##### [Human T-lymphotropic virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_T-lymphotropic_virus) (types I and II)

Human T-Lymphotrophic Virus (HTLV) is able to be passed through breastmilk from mother to child.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 15–17 </sup>[^cite_note-164] The worldwide rate of transmission through breastmilk is estimated to be 3.9–27%, and this risk is increased by high maternal viral load and prolonged periods of breastfeeding.[^cite_note-auto2-165][^cite_note-166] Current data demonstrates that while breastfeeding for less than six months does not, independently, increase risk of HTLV-1 transmission, not breastfeeding during that time *does* decrease risk of transmission.[^cite_note-167] As such, [CDC](6811.md) recommends against breastfeeding when mothers have HTLV Types I or II.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 15–17 </sup>[^cite_note-168] Recognizing the importance of breastfeeding in more resource-poor areas of the world, the [World Health Organization](33583.md) recommends shortening the duration of breastfeeding, or avoiding breastfeeding where possible.[^cite_note-auto2-165]

##### Hemorrhagic viral disease ([Marburg virus](58743.md), [Ebola](40817590.md))

Mothers with Marburg virus or Ebola should not breastfeed their infants or feed them with expressed breastmilk.[^cite_note-CDC-2021f-169][^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 738–753 </sup>

##### Tuberculosis

Infants whose mothers have suspected untreated [tuberculosis](30653.md) (TB) should be isolated from their mothers to reduce the risk of transmission. As such, these infants should not be breastfed during this time and until the mother has been treated appropriately for 2 weeks and is no longer contagious.[^cite_note-CDC-2021f-169][^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 411–313 </sup>[^cite_note-HealthyChildren-2021-170] However, these infants may be fed expressed breastmilk from their mother.[^cite_note-CDC-2021f-169][^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 411–313 </sup>[^cite_note-HealthyChildren-2021-170] Transmission of TB through breastmilk, without an isolated breast infection caused by the Tuberculosis bacteria (*[Mycobacterium tuberculosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycobacterium_tuberculosis)*), has never been documented in the scientific literature.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 411–313 </sup> Mothers who do have an isolated breast infection caused by *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*, termed **tuberculous mastitis**, should not feed their infants with their own breastmilk, even if it is fed by bottle.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 411–313 </sup>

##### Herpes simplex

[Herpes simplex virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herpes_simplex_virus) (HSV) is the virus that causes genital [herpes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herpes) and oral [cold sores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_sore), and it can be very dangerous to infants.[^cite_note-auto1-171] The CDC advises to continue breastfeeding if there are no open/active lesions on the breast and other lesions covered.[^cite_note-auto1-171]

##### [Herpes zoster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herpes_zoster) (chickenpox and shingles)

Varicella zoster is the virus responsible for chickenpox and shingles (also known as herpes zoster).[^cite_note-CDC-2021-172] The [CDC](6811.md) advises that breastfeeding is safe to continue as long as the breasts are clear of lesions, also emphasizing that if pumping or [hand expressing milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hand_expression_of_breast_milk), proper [hand-hygiene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hand_washing) should be used to minimize transfer.[^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 15–17 </sup>[^cite_note-CDC-2021-172]



An infographic from the [World Health Organization](33583.md) on COVID-19 preventive measures to be taken while breastfeeding

##### [COVID-19](63030231.md) (no contraindication)

In May 2020, [WHO](33583.md) and [UNICEF](19698110.md) stressed that the ongoing [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) was not a reason to discontinue breastfeeding. They recommend that women continue to breastfeed during the pandemic, even if they have confirmed or suspected COVID-19, because evidence indicates that it is unlikely that COVID-19 can be transmitted through breast milk.[^cite_note-173] A study published in 2021 found that, while [SARS-CoV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2) [RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNA) may be found in some samples of breastmilk from recently infected mothers, the breastmilk does not contain infectious virus and is not considered a transmission risk factor.[^cite_note-174] Mothers who have suspected or confirmed diagnoses of COVID-19 should thoroughly wash their hands and wear a well-fitting mask before breastfeeding their infant, or express breastmilk and feed the infant by bottle.[^cite_note-175]

### Infantile contraindications

#### [Galactosemia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactosemia)

Galactosemia is a [metabolic disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metabolic_disorder) that prevents the infant from breaking down [galactose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactose), which is one of the two components of [lactose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactose), a type of sugar found in milk. Lactose is also found in breastmilk, so infants with galactosemia should not breastfeed.[^cite_note-pmid20301691-176][^cite_note-Eglash_2020-11]<sup>: 15 </sup>[^cite_note-CDC-2021e-153]

## Methods



[Formula](65330.md) and pumped breastmilk side by side. Note that the formula is of uniform consistency and color, while the expressed breast milk exhibits properties of an organic solution by separating into a layer of fat at the top (the "creamline"), followed by the milk, and then a watery blue-colored layer at the bottom.

### Expressed milk



Manual breast pump

A mother may *express* milk (remove milk from breasts) for [storage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breastmilk_storage_and_handling) and later use. Expression may occur manually with hand expression or by using a [breast pump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_pump).[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 220 </sup>[^cite_note-Eglash-2017-177]

Mothers express milk for multiple reasons. Expressing breast milk can maintain a mother's milk supply when mother and child are apart. A sick baby who is unable to nurse can take expressed milk through a [nasogastric tube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasogastric_tube). Some babies are unable or unwilling to nurse.
