Maternal breastmilk is the food of choice for [premature babies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premature_babies); these infants may be fed maternal milk through tubes, [supplemental nursing systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supplemental_nursing_systems), bottles, spoons, or cups until they develop satisfactory ability to suck and swallow breast milk.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 502–545 </sup> Some women donate expressed breast milk (EBM) to others, either directly or through a [milk bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_milk_bank). This allows mothers who cannot breastfeed to give their baby the benefits of breast milk. While informally-shared breastmilk does carry the nutritional benefits of breastmilk, this breastmilk is most often not pasteurized or screened, and thus carries with it the risk of transmitting diseases or medications that are unsafe for infants.[^cite_note-Sriraman-2018-178] Parents considering directed or informal milk sharing should discuss this option with their doctor, and they should be familiar with the donor's medical history and milk handling practices.[^cite_note-Sriraman-2018-178] Use of informally-shared (unscreened, not pasteurized) milk from an anonymous donor is discouraged by the Academy of Breastfeeding Medicine.[^cite_note-Sriraman-2018-178]

Babies feed differently with artificial nipples than from a breast. With the breast, the infant's tongue massages the milk out rather than sucking, and the nipple does not go as far into the mouth. Drinking from a bottle takes less effort, and the milk may come more quickly, potentially causing the baby to lose desire for the breast. This is often referred to as *nipple confusion* or *nipple preference*.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 241 </sup> While some infants do experience this preference for the bottle, many infants do not and will be able to alternate between bottle and breast without issue.[^cite_note-Lawrence_2021-51]<sup>: 241 </sup>

"Exclusively expressing" and "exclusively pumping" are terms for a mother who exclusively feeds a baby expressed milk. Exclusively pumping is poorly studied in the literature, especially in recent years. However, from available evidence, it appears fairly uncommon, with only approximately 7% of study participants reporting exclusive pumping.[^cite_note-179]

#### Storage of expressed breastmilk

See also: [Breastmilk storage and handling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breastmilk_storage_and_handling)

Breastmilk may be stored for various amounts of time depending on storage temperature and conditions. The content and quality of expressed milk change over time as it is stored, particularly when frozen.[^cite_note-Eglash-2017-177] For example, there is a decrease in the ability of breastmilk to kill bacteria when it is stored in the refrigerator for more than 48 hours.[^cite_note-Eglash-2017-177] Additionally, the quantity of fat, protein, and calories in breastmilk decreases when the milk is frozen for more than 3 months. While several components of breastmilk change over time, inflammatory factors (cytokines) and maternal antibodies, and growth factors are thought to be stable for at least 6 months when the breastmilk is frozen.[^cite_note-Eglash-2017-177] Storage guidelines, according to the [CDC](6811.md), [La Leche League International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Leche_League_International) and the Academy of Breastfeeding Medicine, are noted in the table below.

| Storage Location | Optimal Storage Time | Acceptable Storage Time | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Countertop | 4 hours | 8 hours | [^cite_note-LLLI-2021a-180][^cite_note-Eglash-2017-177] |
| Insulated bag with icepack | 24 hours |  | [^cite_note-LLLI-2021a-180][^cite_note-CDC-2021a-181] |
| Refrigerator | 4 days | 5 days | [^cite_note-Eglash-2017-177][^cite_note-CDC-2021a-181][^cite_note-LLLI-2021a-180] |
| Freezer compartment of minifridge | 2 weeks |  | [^cite_note-LLLI-2021a-180] |
| Standard Freezer | 6 months | 12 months | [^cite_note-Eglash-2017-177][^cite_note-CDC-2021a-181][^cite_note-LLLI-2021a-180] |
| Deep Freezer (usually separate from refrigerator) | 6–12 months |  | [^cite_note-Eglash-2017-177][^cite_note-LLLI-2021a-180] |

#### Breastmilk storage containers

See also: [Breastmilk storage and handling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breastmilk_storage_and_handling)

Expressed breastmilk can be stored in freezer storage bags, containers made specifically for breastmilk, a [supplemental nursing system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supplemental_nursing_system), or a [bottle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_bottle) ready for use. Parents should avoid using storage containers which contain [bisphenol A (BPA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BPA-free).[^cite_note-CDC-2021a-181] Additionally, use of [polyethylene containers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyethylene) have been shown to decrease the immune benefits of breastmilk, including its ability to kill bacteria and the maternal antibodies it contains, by up to 60%.[^cite_note-Eglash-2017-177]

### Shared nursing

Main article: [Wet nurse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_nurse)

It is not only a mother who may breastfeed a child. Parents may hire another person to do so (a [wet nurse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_nurse)), or may share childcare with another mother (cross-nursing). Both of these were common throughout history. It remains popular in some [developing nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developing_nation), including those in Africa, for more than one woman to breastfeed a child. Shared breastfeeding is a risk factor for [HIV](14170.md) infection in infants.[^cite_note-182] Shared nursing can sometimes provoke negative social reactions in the [English-speaking world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English-speaking_world).[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184]

### Tandem nursing

A mother can continue breastfeeding an older sibling while also breastfeeding a new baby; this is called *tandem nursing*. During the late stages of pregnancy, the milk changes to colostrum. While some children continue to breastfeed even with this change, others may [wean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wean). Most mothers can produce enough milk for tandem nursing, but the new baby should be nursed first for at least the first few days after delivery to ensure it receives enough colostrum.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceLawrence2015707–708-185]

Breastfeeding [triplets or larger broods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_birth) is a challenge given babies' varying appetites. Breasts can respond to the demand and produce larger milk quantities; mothers have breastfed triplets successfully.[^cite_note-Grunberg_1992-186][^cite_note-ABA-187][^cite_note-Brown,_S-188]

- Newborn twins being breastfed

  Newborn twins being breastfed

- Woman breastfeeds 2-year-old twins whilst reading them a book.

  Woman breastfeeds 2-year-old twins whilst reading them a book.

- Woman tandem nurses an infant and a toddler.

  Woman tandem nurses an infant and a toddler.

### Re-lactation and induced lactation

See also: [Lactation § Without pregnancy, induced lactation, relactation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactation#Without_pregnancy,_induced_lactation,_relactation)

Re-lactation is the process of restarting breastfeeding.[^cite_note-Wambach-189] In developing countries, mothers may restart breastfeeding after a weaning as part of an [oral rehydration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_rehydration) treatment for [diarrhea](53951.md). In developed countries, re-lactation is common after early medical problems are resolved, or because a mother changes her mind about breastfeeding.

Re-lactation is most easily accomplished with a newborn or with a baby that was previously breastfeeding; if the baby was initially bottle-fed, the baby may refuse to suckle. If the mother has recently stopped breastfeeding, chances are higher that the milk supply will return and be adequate. Although some mothers successfully re-lactate after months-long interruptions, success is higher for shorter interruptions.[^cite_note-Wambach-189]

Techniques to promote lactation include frequent attempts to breastfeed, extensive skin-to-skin contact with the baby, and frequent, long pumping sessions.[^cite_note-Wambach-189] Suckling may be encouraged with a tube filled with infant formula, so that the baby associates suckling at the breast with food. A dropper or syringe without the needle may be used to place milk onto the breast while the baby suckles. The mother should allow the infant to suckle at least ten times during 24 hours, and more times if the baby is interested. These times can include every two hours, whenever the baby seems interested, longer at each breast, and when the baby is sleepy, when they might suckle more readily. In keeping with increasing contact between mother and child, including increasing skin-to-skin contact, grandmothers should pull back and help in other ways. Later on, grandmothers can again provide more direct care for the infant.[^cite_note-TreatmentOfDiarrhoea-WHO-2005-190]

These techniques require the mother's commitment over a period of weeks or months. However, even when lactation is established, the supply may not be large enough to breastfeed exclusively. A supportive social environment improves the likelihood of success.[^cite_note-Wambach-189] As the mother's milk production increases, other feeding can decrease. Parents and other family members should watch the baby's weight gain and urine output to assess nutritional adequacy.[^cite_note-TreatmentOfDiarrhoea-WHO-2005-190]

A WHO manual for physicians and senior health workers, citing a 1992 source, states: "If a baby has been breastfeeding sometimes, the breastmilk supply increases in a few days. If a baby has stopped breastfeeding, it may take 1–2 weeks or more before much breastmilk comes."[^cite_note-TreatmentOfDiarrhoea-WHO-2005-190]

Induced lactation, also called *adoptive lactation*, is the process of starting breastfeeding in a woman who did not give birth.[^cite_note-Wambach-189] This usually requires the adoptive mother to take hormones and other drugs to stimulate breast development and promote milk production. In some cultures, breastfeeding an adoptive child creates [milk kinship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milk_kinship) that builds community bonds across class and other hierarchical bonds.[^cite_note-Wambach-189]

## Health effects



A mother using a [baby carrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_carrier) to support a feeding baby in Rio de Janeiro

Breastfeeding support is universal among major health and children's organizations. WHO states, "Breast milk is the ideal food for the healthy growth and development of infants; breastfeeding is also an integral part of the reproductive process with important implications for the health of mothers."[^cite_note-World_Health_Organization-191]

Breastfeeding is associated with a lowered risk of several diseases in both mothers and babies.[^cite_note-192] Comparing infants that were exclusively breastfed for at least 3 months with never-breastfed infants, the [American Academy of Pediatrics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Academy_of_Pediatrics) reported that in the first year of life breastfed babies averaged about \$400 in savings of health care costs.[^cite_note-193]

### Baby

Infants who are not breastfed are at mildly increased risk of developing lower [respiratory infection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_infection), [ear infections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otitis_media), [bacteremia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacteremia), [bacterial meningitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacterial_meningitis), [botulism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botulism), [urinary tract infection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urinary_tract_infection) and [necrotizing enterocolitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Necrotizing_enterocolitis).[^cite_note-WHO_2007-194][^cite_note-AHRQ_2007-195] Breastfeeding may protect against [sudden infant death syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudden_infant_death_syndrome),[^cite_note-196] [insulin-dependent diabetes mellitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insulin-dependent_diabetes_mellitus), [Crohn's disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crohn's_disease), [ulcerative colitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulcerative_colitis), childhood [lymphoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lymphoma), allergic diseases, digestive diseases,[^cite_note-Gartner_2005-68] obesity, and childhood leukemia.[^cite_note-197] It is hard however to distinguish the importance of breastfeeding per se and other correlated socioeconomic factors (breastfeeding is more frequent in richer families with higher educations). Comparing breastfed and non-breastfed siblings in a given family drastically decreases the association between breastfeeding and long-term child well-being.[^cite_note-Colen_2014-49]

#### Growth

The average breastfed baby doubles its birth weight in 5–6 months. By one year, a typical breastfed baby weighs about 2+1⁄2 times its birth weight. At one year, breastfed babies tend to be leaner than formula-fed babies, which improves long-term health.[^cite_note-Kingdom_of_Bahrain_Ministry_of_Health_Health_Promotion_Council-198]

The Davis Area Research on Lactation, Infant Nutrition and Growth (DARLING) study reported that breastfed and formula-fed groups had similar weight gain during the first 3 months. Breastfed babies began to drop below the median beginning at 6 to 8 months and were significantly lower in weight than the formula-fed group between 6 and 18 months. Length gain and head circumference values were similar between groups, suggesting that the breastfed babies were leaner.[^cite_note-Pediatrics_Journal-199]

#### Infections

Main article: [Human milk immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_milk_immunity)

Breast milk contains several [anti-infective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infection_control) factors such as [bile salt stimulated lipase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bile_salt_dependent_lipase) (protecting against [amoebic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amoeba) infections) and [lactoferrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactoferrin) (which binds to iron and inhibits the growth of [intestinal bacteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gut_flora)).[^cite_note-pmid10394490-200][^cite_note-pmid10394491-201]

Exclusive breastfeeding until six months of age helps to protect an infant from gastrointestinal infections in both developing and industrialized countries. The risk of death due to diarrhea and other infections increases when babies are either partially breastfed or not breastfed at all.[^cite_note-WHO20142-4] Infants who are exclusively breastfed for the first six months are less likely to die of [gastrointestinal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastrointestinal) infections than infants who switched from exclusive to partial breastfeeding at three to four months.[^cite_note-Kramer_2012-6]

During breastfeeding, approximately 0.25–0.5 grams per day of secretory [IgA antibodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IgA_antibodies) pass to the baby via milk.[^cite_note-202][^cite_note-CDC-203] This is one of the important features of colostrum.[^cite_note-Immune-204] The main target for these antibodies are probably microorganisms in the baby's [intestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intestine). The rest of the body displays some uptake of IgA,[^cite_note-205] but this amount is relatively small.[^cite_note-206]

Maternal vaccinations while breastfeeding are safe for almost all vaccines. Additionally, the mother's immunity obtained by vaccination against [tetanus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetanus), [diphtheria](58937.md), [whooping cough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whooping_cough), and [influenza](19572217.md) can protect the baby from these diseases, and breastfeeding can reduce the fever rate after infant immunization. However, [smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox) and [yellow fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_fever) vaccines increase the risk of infants developing [vaccinia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccinia) and [encephalitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encephalitis).[^cite_note-breastfedvaccine-207][^cite_note-breastfeddrug-208]

Several studies have suggested that breast milk can pass antibodies to the infant for as long as the child continues to nurse. The antibodies may be in the mother's system as a result of being ill, or they may be acquired by drinking milk from a mother who has recently been vaccinated for a particular disease. One small study done on nursing mothers who had received the COVID-19 vaccine found that breastmilk continued to contain antibodies for as long as 80 days after receiving the vaccine.[^cite_note-209]

#### Mortality

The World Health Organization reports that babies who receive no breast milk are almost six times more likely to die by the age of one month than those who are partially or fully breastfed. Access to healthcare is the single critical determinant of survival or death for the infant.[^cite_note-210]

#### Childhood obesity

Main article: [Childhood obesity](4572582.md)

The protective effect of breastfeeding against obesity is consistent, though small, across many studies.[^cite_note-WHO_2007-194][^cite_note-AHRQ_2007-195][^cite_note-211] A 2013 [longitudinal study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longitudinal_study) reported less obesity at ages two and four years among infants who were breastfed for at least four months.[^cite_note-212]

#### Allergic diseases

In children who are at risk for developing allergic diseases (defined as at least one parent or sibling having [atopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atopy)), atopic syndrome can be prevented or delayed through 4-month exclusive breastfeeding, though these benefits may not persist.[^cite_note-pmid18166574-213]

#### Backwash effect

The backwash effect in breastfeeding refers to the process in which an infant's saliva flows back into the mother's breast during nursing. This backward flow may introduce the baby's saliva into the mammary gland, potentially prompting the mother's body to produce tailored immune responses in her breast milk to meet the infant's specific needs.[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-hassiotou2013-215]

#### Other health effects

Breastfeeding may reduce the risk of necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC) in premature babies.[^cite_note-AHRQ_2007-195]

Breastfeeding or introduction of gluten while breastfeeding does not protect against [celiac disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celiac_disease) among at-risk children. Breast milk of healthy human mothers who eat [gluten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gluten)-containing foods presents high levels of non-degraded [gliadin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gliadin) (the main gluten protein). Early introduction of traces of gluten in babies to potentially induce tolerance does not reduce the risk of developing celiac disease. Delaying the introduction of gluten does not prevent, but is associated with a delayed onset of the disease.[^cite_note-SzajewskaShamir-216][^cite_note-BethuneKhosla2008-217]

About 14 to 19 percent of leukemia cases may be prevented by breastfeeding for six months or longer.[^cite_note-218] However, breastfeeding is also the primary cause of [adult T-cell leukemia/lymphoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adult_T-cell_leukemia/lymphoma), as the [HTLV-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HTLV-1) virus is transmitted through breastmilk.[^cite_note-219]

Breastfeeding is associated with a lower chance of developing [diabetes mellitus type 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes_mellitus_type_1) in the offspring.[^cite_note-AHRQ_2007-195] Breastfed babies also appear to have a lower likelihood of developing [diabetes mellitus type 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes_mellitus_type_2) later in life.[^cite_note-WHO_2007-194][^cite_note-AHRQ_2007-195][^cite_note-pmid17093156-220]

Breastfeeding may decrease the risk of [cardiovascular disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease) in later life, as indicated by lower [cholesterol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cholesterol) and [C-reactive protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-reactive_protein) levels in breastfed adult women.[^cite_note-WHO_2007-194] Breastfed infants have somewhat lower blood pressure later in life, but it is unclear how much practical benefit this provides.[^cite_note-WHO_2007-194][^cite_note-AHRQ_2007-195]

A 1998 study suggested that breastfed babies have a better chance of good dental health than formula-fed infants because of the developmental effects of breastfeeding on the [oral cavity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_cavity) and [airway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airway). It was thought that with fewer [malocclusions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malocclusion), breastfed children may have a reduced need for [orthodontic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodontic) intervention. The report suggested that children with a well-rounded, "U-shaped" [dental arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dental_arch), which is found more commonly in breastfed children, may have fewer problems with snoring and [sleep apnea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleep_apnea) in later life.[^cite_note-221] A 2016 review found that breastfeeding protected against malocclusions.[^cite_note-Victora_2016-10]

Breastfeeding duration has been correlated with child maltreatment outcomes, including neglect and sexual abuse.[^cite_note-Kremer_2018-222]

#### Intelligence

It is unclear whether breastfeeding improves [intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence) later in life. Several studies found no relationship after controlling for [confounding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confounding) factors like maternal intelligence (smarter mothers were more likely to breastfeed their babies).[^cite_note-AHRQ_2007-195][^cite_note-pmid17020911-223] However, other studies concluded that breastfeeding was associated with increased cognitive development in childhood, although the cause may be increased mother–child interaction rather than nutrition.[^cite_note-WHO_2007-194][^cite_note-224]

### Mother

#### Maternal bond

[Oxytocin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxytocin), a hormone released during breastfeeding, may play a role in maternal-infant attachment and bonding, potentially via decreased anxiety and stress.[^cite_note-225]

#### Fertility

Main article: [Lactational amenorrhea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactational_amenorrhea)

Exclusive breastfeeding usually delays the return of fertility through [lactational amenorrhea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactational_amenorrhea),[^cite_note-Chowdhury_2015-226][^cite_note-227] although it does not provide reliable [birth control](18978770.md). Mothers may not [ovulate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovulate) or have regular periods during the entire lactation period. The non-ovulating period varies by individual. This has been used as natural contraception, with greater than 98% effectiveness during the first six months after birth if specific nursing behaviors are followed.[^cite_note-Price_\&Robinson_2004-228]

#### Postpartum bleeding

During the third stage of labor, the time between the delivery of the baby and the passage of the placenta, and the fourth, the final stage of birth, excessive blood loss can endanger the life of the mother. When the newborn nurses, the mother secretes [oxytocin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxytocin), which causes the uterus to contract and reduce blood loss. Nursing also causes the uterus to cramp for several days postpartum, helping it to return to its pre-pregnancy size. Some women report moderate to severe pain, especially women who have given birth several times, during a nursing session for the first few days following delivery.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230]

#### Weight retention

It is unclear whether breastfeeding causes mothers to lose weight after giving birth.[^cite_note-Chowdhury_2015-226][^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232][^cite_note-Feltner_2018-233] The National Institutes of Health states that it may help with weight loss.[^cite_note-234]

#### Chronic conditions

Breastfeeding is also associated with a lower risk of type 2 diabetes among mothers who practice it.[^cite_note-Aune_2014-235] Longer duration of breastfeeding is associated with reduced risk of hypertension.[^cite_note-Feltner_2018-233]

For breastfeeding women, long-term health benefits include reduced risk of [breast cancer](70547.md), [ovarian cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovarian_cancer), and [endometrial cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endometrial_cancer).[^cite_note-Chowdhury_2015-226][^cite_note-236] According to the [American Heart Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Heart_Association), breastfeeding also reduces the risk of maternal heart disease and stroke.[^cite_note-237]

A 2011 review found it unclear whether breastfeeding affects the risk of [postpartum depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postpartum_depression).[^cite_note-238] Later reviews have found tentative evidence of a lower risk among mothers who successfully breastfeed, though it is unknown whether breastfeeding decreases depression, or whether depression decreases breastfeeding.[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240]

#### Dysphoric milk ejection reflex

Main article: [Dysphoric milk ejection reflex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dysphoric_milk_ejection_reflex)

Dysphoric milk ejection reflex (D-MER) is a condition in which breastfeeding women develop negative emotions that begin just before the milk letdown reflex and last less than a few minutes. It may recur with every letdown, including unexpected letdowns when the baby is not feeding. It presents as an emotional reaction but may also produce physical feelings such as nausea. It is different from postpartum depression and other known psychological conditions. A 2019 study reported a prevalence rate of 9.1%. As of 2021, very little research has been done, and many health care providers and lactation practitioners remain barely able to recognize the syndrome. An October 2021 review of literature published to that date suggested that the lack of up-to-date information "makes it necessary to educate mothers because educated mothers are usually better at handling postpartum situations if they are prepared in advance." There is as yet no medication to treat the symptoms although women have reported that they have found it of benefit to learn that they are not alone and that the symptoms were not "just in their head".[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243]

## Social factors

Main article: [Breastfeeding difficulties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breastfeeding_difficulties)

The majority of mothers intend to breastfeed at birth. Many factors can disrupt this intent. Research done in the US shows that obstetricians rarely provide information about breastfeeding during their prenatal visits, and some health professionals incorrectly believe that commercially prepared formula is nutritionally equivalent to breast milk.[^cite_note-U.S._Department_of_Health_and_Human_Services-244] Many hospitals have instituted practices that encourage breastfeeding, however a 2012 survey in the US found that 24% of maternity services were still providing supplements of commercial infant formula as a general practice in the first 48 hours after birth.[^cite_note-Johnston_2012-5] *The [Surgeon General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surgeon_General_of_the_United_States)'s Call to Action to Support Breastfeeding* attempts to educate practitioners.[^cite_note-pmid21886320-245]

### Social support

See also: [Postpartum confinement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postpartum_confinement)

A review found that when effective forms of support are offered to women, exclusive breastfeeding and duration of breastfeeding are increased. Characteristics of effective support include ongoing, face-to-face support tailored to fit their needs. It may be offered by lay/peer supporters, professional supporters, or a combination of both.[^cite_note-246] This review contrasts with another large review that looked at education programs alone, which found no conclusive evidence of initiation of breastfeeding or the proportion of women breastfeeding either exclusively or partially at 3 months and 6 months.[^cite_note-Lumbiganon_2016-247]

Positive social support in essential relationships of new mothers plays a central role in promoting breastfeeding outside of medical centers. Social support can come in many incarnations, including tangible, affectionate, social interactions, and emotional and informational support. An increase in these capacities of support has shown to greatly positively effect breastfeeding rates, especially among women with education below a high school level.[^cite_note-Laugen_2016-248] Some mothers who have used lactation rooms have taken to leaving sticky notes to not only thank the businesses that have provided them but to support, encourage, and praise the nursing mothers who use them.[^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-249]

In the social circles surrounding the mother, support is most crucial from the male partner, the mother's mother, and her family and friends.[^cite_note-250] Research has shown that the closest relationships to the mother have the strongest impact on breastfeeding rates, while negative perspectives on breastfeeding from close relatives hinder its prevalence.[^cite_note-Laugen_2016-248]

- Mother – Adolescence is a risk factor for low breastfeeding rates, although classes, books, and personal counseling (professional or lay) can help compensate.

  [^cite_note-Woods_2013-251]

  &#x20;Some women fear that breastfeeding will negatively impact the look of their breasts. However, a 2008 study found that breastfeeding did not affect the breasts; other factors did contribute to ["drooping" of the breasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptosis_\(breasts\)), such as advanced age, number of pregnancies, and smoking behavior.

  [^cite_note-252]

- Partner – Partners may lack knowledge of breastfeeding and their role in the practice.

- [Wet nursing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_nursing) – Social and cultural attitudes towards breastfeeding in the African-American community are also influenced by the legacy of forced wet-nursing during slavery.

  [^cite_note-Lutenbacher_2016-253]

#### Maternity leave

Work is the most commonly cited reason for not breastfeeding.[^cite_note-254] In 2012, [Save the Children](436885.md) examined [maternity leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternity_leave) laws, ranking 36 industrialized countries according to their support for breastfeeding. Norway ranked first, while the United States came in last.[^cite_note-savethechildren.org-255] [Maternity leave in the US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternity_leave_in_the_United_States) varies widely, including by state. The United States does not mandate paid maternity leave for any employee; however, the [Family Medical Leave Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_Medical_Leave_Act) (FMLA) guarantees qualifying mothers up to 12 weeks of unpaid leave, although most US mothers resume work earlier. A large 2011 study found that women who returned to work at or after 13 weeks after childbirth were more likely to breastfeed beyond three months.[^cite_note-256]

### Healthcare

#### Caesarean section

Women are less likely to start breastfeeding after [caesarean delivery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesarean_section) compared with vaginal delivery.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] Though the stress of a caesarean section (c-section) may impact let-down and flow, and though time for recovery immediately after surgery may be needed, women who give birth via c-section are still able to breastfeed.[^cite_note-\:5-259] Generally, the benefits of breastfeeding outweigh the harms posed by pain medications following surgery.[^cite_note-\:5-259] Mothers may need to experiment with different [breastfeeding positions](#Position) to avoid putting pressure on the incision.[^cite_note-\:5-259]

#### Breast surgery

Breastfeeding can generally be attempted after breast augmentation or reduction surgery;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceLawrence2015615–616-260] however, prior breast surgery is a risk factor for [low milk supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_milk_supply).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceLawrence2015231-261]

A 2014 review found that women who have [breast implant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_implant) surgery were less likely to breastfeed exclusively; however, it was based on only three small studies, and the reasons for the correlation were not clear.[^cite_note-262] A large follow-up study done in 2014 found a reduced rate of breastfeeding in women who had undergone breast augmentation surgery; however, again, the reasons were unclear. The authors suggested that women contemplating augmentation should be provided with information related to the rates of successful breastfeeding as part of informed decision-making when contemplating surgery.[^cite_note-263]

Prior [breast reduction surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_reduction_surgery) is strongly associated with an increased probability of [low milk supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_milk_supply) due to disruption to tissues and nerves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceLawrence2015231,_616-264] Some surgical techniques for breast reduction appear to be more successful than others in preserving the tissues that generate and channel milk to the nipple. A 2017 review found that women were more likely to have success with breastfeeding with these techniques.[^cite_note-265]

#### Medications

Breastfeeding mothers should inform their healthcare provider about all of the medications they are taking, including herbal products. Nursing mothers may be immunized and may take most [over-the-counter drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Over-the-counter_drugs) and [prescription drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prescription_drugs) without risk to the baby but certain drugs, including some [painkillers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analgesic) and some [psychiatric drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychiatric_drugs), may pose a risk.[^cite_note-266]

The [US National Library of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_National_Library_of_Medicine) publishes "LactMed," an up-to-date online database of information on drugs and lactation. Geared to both healthcare practitioners and nursing mothers, LactMed contains over 450 drug records with information such as potential drug effects and alternative drugs to consider.[^cite_note-breastfeddrug-208][^cite_note-American_Academy_of_Pediatrics-267]

Some substances in the mother's food and drink are passed to the baby through breast milk, including [mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_\(element\)) (found in some [carnivorous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivorous) fish),[^cite_note-268] [caffeine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caffeine),[^cite_note-269] and [bisphenol A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bisphenol_A).[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271]

#### Medical conditions

Undiagnosed maternal [celiac disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coeliac_disease) may cause a short duration of the breastfeeding period. Treatment with the [gluten-free diet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gluten-free_diet) can increase its duration and restore it to the average value of the healthy women.[^cite_note-TersigniCastellani2014-272]

Mothers with all types of [diabetes mellitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes_mellitus) normally use insulin to control their blood sugar, as the safety of other [antidiabetic drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antidiabetic_drugs) while breastfeeding is unknown.[^cite_note-273]

Women with [polycystic ovary syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polycystic_ovary_syndrome), which is associated with some hormonal differences and obesity, may have greater difficulty with producing a sufficient supply to support exclusive breastfeeding, especially during the first weeks.[^cite_note-274]

### Ethnicity and socioeconomic status

The rates of breastfeeding in the African-American community remain much lower than those of any other race, for a variety of proposed reasons. These include the legacy of [wet nursing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_nursing) during slavery, higher rates of poor perinatal health, higher stress levels, less access to support, and less flexibility in the workplace.[^cite_note-Johnson-275] While for other races, as socio-economic status increases, rates of breastfeeding also go up, for the African-American community, breastfeeding rates remain consistently low regardless of socio-economic class.[^cite_note-Reeves_2015-276]

There are also racial disparities in access to maternity care practices that support breastfeeding. In the US, primarily African-American neighborhoods are more likely to have facilities (such as hospitals and female healthcare clinics) that do not support breastfeeding, contributing to the low rate of breastfeeding in the African-American community. Comparing facilities in primarily African American neighborhoods to ones in primarily European-American neighborhoods, the rates of practices that support or discourage breastfeeding were: limited use of supplements (13.1% compared with 25.8%) and rooming-in (27.7–39.4%)[^cite_note-277]

Low-income mothers are more likely to have unintended pregnancies.[^cite_note-surgeongeneral.gov-278] Mothers whose pregnancies are unintended are less likely to breastfeed.[^cite_note-HP2020FP-279]

Especially the combination of powdered formula with unclean water can be very harmful to the health of babies. In the late 1970s, there was a boycott against Nestle due to the large number of baby deaths due to formula. [Dr. Michele Barry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michele_Barry) explains that breastfeeding is most imperative in poverty environments due to the lack of access to clean water for formula. A Lancet study in 2016 found that universal breastfeeding would prevent the deaths of 800,000 children as well as save \$300,000,000.[^cite_note-Rabin_2018-280]

### Social acceptance

Main article: [Breastfeeding in public](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breastfeeding_in_public)



Sign for a private nursing area at a museum using the [international breastfeeding symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_breastfeeding_symbol)

Some women feel discomfort when breastfeeding in public.[^cite_note-Boyer_2011-281] Public breastfeeding may be forbidden in some [places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisdiction), not addressed by law in others, and a legal right in others. Even given a legal right, some mothers are reluctant to breastfeed,[^cite_note-Wolf_2008-282][^cite_note-Vance_2005-283] while others may object to the practice.[^cite_note-Jordan_2002-284]

It is estimated that around 63% of mothers worldwide have publicly breastfed.[^cite_note-285] The media have reported several incidents in which workers or members of the public have objected to or forbidden women breastfeeding.[^cite_note-286] Some mothers avoid the negative attention and choose to move to another location. But some mothers have protested their treatment and have taken legal action or engaged in protests.[^cite_note-287] Protests have included a public [boycott](18591996.md) of the offender's business, organizing a "nurse-in" or a breastfeeding [flash mob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flash_mob), in which groups of nursing mothers gather at the location where the complaint originated and nursed their babies at the same time. In response, some companies have apologised and instituted reforms.[^cite_note-legoland-288]

The use of infant formula was thought to be a way for Western culture to adapt to negative perceptions of breastfeeding.[^cite_note-Hausman,_B._L._2007-289] The breast pump offered a way for mothers to supply breast milk with most of formula feeding's convenience and without enduring possible disapproval of nursing.[^cite_note-Boyer,_K._2010-290] Some may object to breastfeeding because of the implicit association between infant feeding and sex.[^cite_note-291] These negative cultural connotations may reduce breastfeeding duration.[^cite_note-Wolf_2008-282][^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293][^cite_note-Spencer_2015-294] Maternal guilt and shame are often affected by how a mother feeds an infant. These emotions occur in both bottle- and breast-feeding mothers, although for different reasons. Bottle-feeding mothers may feel they should be breastfeeding.[^cite_note-Taylor_Wallace_2012-295] Conversely, breastfeeding mothers may feel forced to feed in uncomfortable circumstances. Some may see breastfeeding as "indecent, disgusting, animalistic, sexual, and even possibly a perverse act."[^cite_note-Forbes_2003-296] Advocates (known by the [neologism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neologism) "lactivists") use "[nurse-ins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sit-in)" to show support for breastfeeding in public.[^cite_note-Boyer_2011-281] One study that approached the subject from a feminist viewpoint suggested that both nursing and non-nursing mothers often feel maternal guilt and shame, with formula-feeding mothers feeling that they are not living up to the ideals of motherhood and nursing mothers concerned that they are transgressing "cultural expectations regarding feminine modesty." The authors advocate that women be provided with education on breastfeeding's benefits as well as problem-solving skills,[^cite_note-Taylor_Wallace_2012-295] however, there is no conclusive evidence that breastfeeding education alone improves initiation of breastfeeding or the proportion of women breastfeeding either exclusively or partially at 3 months and 6 months.[^cite_note-Lumbiganon_2016-247]

### Location

| Globe icon. | The examples and perspectives in this section **deal primarily with the United States and the United Kingdom and do not represent a [worldwide view](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:WikiProject_Countering_systemic_bias) of the subject**. You may [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Breastfeeding), discuss the issue on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Breastfeeding), or create a new section, as appropriate. *(December 2024)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

In the [United States](3434750.md), all fifty [states](18618239.md), along with the [District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia), [Puerto Rico](23041.md), and the [U.S. Virgin Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Virgin_Islands), have laws that allow a mother to breastfeed a baby in any public or private location.[^cite_note-297] In that country, the Friendly Airports for Mothers (FAM) Act was signed into law in 2019, and the requirements went into effect in 2021. This law requires that all large and medium hub airports in the U.S. provide a private, non-bathroom lactation space in each terminal building.[^cite_note-298]

Some commercial establishments in the U.S. provide breastfeeding rooms, although laws generally specify that mothers may breastfeed anywhere without requiring a special area. Despite these laws, many women in the United States continue to be publicly shamed or asked to refrain from breastfeeding in public.[^cite_note-299] In the [United Kingdom](31717.md), the [Equality Act 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_Act_2010) makes the prevention of breastfeeding in any public place [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-discrimination_law) under the law.[^cite_note-uk-300] In [Scotland](26994.md), it is a criminal offense for one to attempt to prevent another from feeding a child under 24 months in public.[^cite_note-uk-300]

While laws in the U.S. were passed in 2010 which required that nursing mothers who had returned to work be given a non-bathroom space to express milk and a reasonable break time to do so, as of 2016 the majority of American women still did not have access to both accommodations.[^cite_note-Breastfeeding:_The_Illusion_of_Choi-301]

In 2014, newly elected [Pope Francis](1687680.md) drew worldwide commentary when he encouraged mothers to breastfeed babies in church. During a papal [baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism), he said that mothers "should not stand on ceremony" if their children were hungry. "If they are hungry, mothers, feed them, without thinking twice," he said, smiling. "Because they are the most important people here."[^cite_note-302]

## Prevalence



exclusive breastfeeding rate



Percentage of US infants breastfeeding by month since birth in 2008.\
Dotted line: Exclusive breastfeeding\
Dashed line Any breastfeeding\
\* Estimated at 7 days after birth

In 2012, 37.1% of infants under six months of age were breastfeeding exclusively, which rose to 48% in 2022. The Sustainable Development Goals target is to achieve 70% by 2030.[^cite_note-WHO20142-4][^cite_note-\:3-303]

In the United States, the rate of women beginning to breastfeed was 76% in 2009, increasing to 83% in 2015, with 58% still breastfeeding at 6 months, although only 25% were still breastfeeding exclusively.[^cite_note-CDC2018Rate-304] African-American women have persistently low rates of breastfeeding compared to White and Hispanic American women. In 2014, 58.1% of African-American women breastfeed in the early postpartum period, compared to 77.7% of White women and 80.6% of Hispanic women.[^cite_note-Reeves_2015-276] In 2019, 84.1% of U.S women giving birth initiated breastfeeding, with 87.4%, 85.5%, 73.6%, 90.3% and 83.1% of Hispanic, White, African-American, Asian and Multiracial mothers initiating, respectively. Rates of initiation among African-American mothers varied widely by state, with lows under 53% and highs over 90%.[^cite_note-305]

Breastfeeding rates in different parts of China vary considerably.[^cite_note-XuQiu2009-306]

Rates in the United Kingdom were the lowest in the world in 2015, with only 0.5% of mothers still breastfeeding at a year, while in Germany, 23% are doing so, 56% in Brazil, and 99% in Senegal.[^cite_note-307]

In Australia, for children born in 2004, more than 90% were initially breastfed.[^cite_note-308] In Canada, for children born in 2005–06, more than 50% were only breastfed, and more than 15% received both breastmilk and other liquids by the age of 3 months.[^cite_note-309]

## History

Main article: [History and culture of breastfeeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_and_culture_of_breastfeeding)

See also: [Wet nurse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_nurse)

In the [Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) and [Roman Empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), women usually fed only their own children. However, breastfeeding began to be seen as something too common to be done by royalty. Wet nurses were employed to breastfeed the children of the royal families. This extended over time, particularly in western Europe, where [noble women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobility) often used wet nurses. Lower-class women breastfed their infants and used a wet nurse only if they were unable to feed their own infant. Attempts were made in 15th-century Europe to use cow or goat milk, but these attempts were unsuccessful. In the 18th century, flour or cereal mixed with broth was introduced as a substitute for breastfeeding, but this provided inadequate nutrition. The appearance of improved infant formulas in the mid-19th century and their increased use caused a decrease in breastfeeding rates, which accelerated after [World War II](32927.md). For some in the US, Canada, and the UK, breastfeeding was seen as uncultured. From the 1960s onwards, breastfeeding experienced a revival that continued into the 2000s, though negative attitudes towards the practice were still entrenched in some countries up to the 1990s.[^cite_note-naoo-310]

## Society and culture



*Macierzyństwo* ('Maternity'), a 1902 painting by [Stanisław Wyspiański](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_Wyspia%C5%84ski)

### Financial considerations

Breastfeeding is less costly than alternatives, but the mother generally must eat more food than would otherwise be necessary. In the US, the extra money spent on food (about US \$18 each week) is usually about half the cost of infant formula.[^cite_note-APP_19972-13] According to the CDC, breastfeeding mothers need an extra 450 to 500 calories per day compared to their pre-pregnancy caloric intake.[^cite_note-311]

Breastfeeding reduces health care costs and the cost of caring for sick babies. Parents of breastfed babies are less likely to miss work and lose income because their babies are sick.[^cite_note-APP_19972-13] Looking at three of the most common infant illnesses, lower respiratory tract illnesses, [otitis media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otitis_media), and gastrointestinal illness, one study compared infants who had been exclusively breastfed for at least three months to those who had not. It found that in the first year of life, there were 2033 excess office visits, 212 excess days of hospitalization, and 609 excess prescriptions for these three illnesses per 1000 never-breastfed infants compared with 1000 infants exclusively breastfed for at least 3 months.[^cite_note-Ball_1999-312][^cite_note-313] However, in a study of over 140,000 newborns in the first month of life, exclusively breastfed newborns had higher hospital readmission rates than those exclusively formula fed, and those exclusively breastfed also had more neonatal outpatient visits compared to those exclusively formula fed.[^cite_note-314]

### Breastfeeding in humanitarian emergencies

During [humanitarian crises](1251700.md), conflicts, forced displacement, and natural disasters, mothers and caregivers face additional difficulties in maintaining adequate breastfeeding practices.[^cite_note-315] They may experience physical and mental exhaustion, lack safe and private spaces for on-demand breastfeeding, and have limited social support.[^cite_note-316] In many of these situations, unsolicited donations of [breast milk substitutes](65330.md) may be distributed through well-intentioned but poorly informed solidarity initiatives. Relying on these products often interrupts breast milk production and creates dependence on formula milk, whose use carries critical health risks in settings where clean water and hygiene materials are not fully accessible.

Following a key meeting hosted by the International Baby Food Action Network (IBFAN) in 1995, the Infant Feeding in Emergencies (IFE) Core Group was established in 1999 to address policy guidance and capacity-building on infant and young child feeding in emergencies.[^cite_note-317] Over the years, and in collaboration with many other [humanitarian organisations](1064581.md), the IFE Core Group has issued key recommendations aimed at monitoring and controlling unsolicited donations of breast milk substitutes, protecting early initiation of breastfeeding, providing food or financial assistance to caregivers, safeguarding infants who depend exclusively on formula milk, establishing and maintaining community support spaces for infant and young child feeding,[^cite_note-318] and fostering inter-sectoral coordination to promote a positive environment for breastfeeding.[^cite_note-319]

### Criticism of breastfeeding advocacy



"See It", a project by [Fiann Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiann_Paul) dedicated to promoting awareness of breastfeeding in [Reykjavík, Iceland](25798.md), in 2011

[^cite_note-320]

There are controversies and ethical considerations surrounding the means used by public campaigns which attempt to increase breastfeeding rates, relating to pressure put on women, and potential feelings of guilt and shame of women who fail to breastfeed; and social condemnation of women who use formula.[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324] In addition to this, there is also the moral question as to what degree the state or medical community can interfere with a person's self-determination: for example in the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) the law requires women to breastfeed babies for at least 2 years and allows husbands to sue them if they do not.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326]

It is widely assumed that if women's healthcare providers encourage them to breastfeed, those who choose not to will experience more guilt. Evidence does not support this assumption. On the contrary, a study on the effects of prenatal breastfeeding counselling found that those who had received such counselling and chosen to formula-feed denied experiencing feelings of guilt. Women were equally comfortable with their subsequent choices for feeding their infant, regardless of whether they had received encouragement to breastfeed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceLawrence2015210–211-327]

Preventing a situation where women are denied agency or stigmatized for formula use is also seen as important. In 2018, in the UK, a policy statement from the [Royal College of Midwives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_College_of_Midwives) said that women should be supported and not stigmatized if, after being given advice and information, they choose to formula feed.[^cite_note-328]

### Social marketing

Social marketing is a marketing approach intended to change people's behavior to benefit both individuals and society.[^cite_note-329] When applied to breastfeeding promotion, social marketing works to provide positive messages and images of breastfeeding to increase visibility. Social marketing in the context of breastfeeding has shown efficacy in media campaigns.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333][^cite_note-CDC_Breatfeeeing_Guide-334][^cite_note-Wakefield_2010-335] Some oppose the marketing of infant formula, especially in developing countries. They are concerned that mothers who use formula will stop breastfeeding and become dependent upon substitutes that are unaffordable or less safe.[^cite_note-Moorhead_2007-336][^cite_note-337] Through efforts including the [Nestlé boycott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestl%C3%A9_boycott), they have advocated for bans on free samples of infant formula and for the adoption of pro-breastfeeding codes such as the [International Code of Marketing of Breast-milk Substitutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Code_of_Marketing_of_Breast-milk_Substitutes) by the [World Health Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Health_Assembly) in 1981 and the Innocenti Declaration by WHO and UNICEF policy-makers in August 1990.[^cite_note-Moorhead_2007-336] Additionally, formula companies have spent millions internationally on campaigns to promote the use of formula as an alternative to mother's milk.[^cite_note-338] Giving out gift bags that contain infant formula to women as they leave the hospital is also a marketing strategy. The U.S. Government Accountability Office has reported that women who receive formula samples at discharge are associated with lower breastfeeding rates than those who do not receive gift bags.[^cite_note-339]

### Baby Friendly Hospital Initiative

Main article: [Baby Friendly Hospital Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_Friendly_Hospital_Initiative)

The Baby Friendly Hospital Initiative (BFHI) is a program launched by the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) in conjunction with [UNICEF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNICEF_UK) to promote infant feeding and maternal bonding through certified hospitals and birthing centers. BFHI was developed as a response to the influence held by formula companies in private and public maternal health care. The initiative has two core tenets: the Ten Steps to Successful Breastfeeding and the [International Code of Marketing of Breast-milk Substitutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Code_of_Marketing_of_Breast-milk_Substitutes).[^cite_note-340] The BFHI has especially targeted hospitals and birthing centers in the developing world, as these facilities are most at risk to the detrimental effects of reduced breastfeeding rates. As of 2018, 530 hospitals in the United States hold the "Baby-Friendly" title in all 50 states. Globally, there are more than 20,000 "Baby-Friendly" hospitals worldwide in over 150 countries.[^cite_note-341]

### Representation on television

The first depiction of breastfeeding on television was in the children's program *[Sesame Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sesame_Street)*, in 1977.[^cite_note-Sen_2018-342] With few exceptions since that time, breastfeeding on television has either been portrayed as strange, disgusting, or a source of comedy, or it has been omitted entirely in favor of bottle feeding.[^cite_note-Sen_2018-342]

### Religion



[Ilkhanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate) prince [Ghazan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazan) being breastfed

See also: [Milk kinship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milk_kinship) and [Mahram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahram)

In some cultures, people who have been breastfed by the same woman are milk-siblings who are equal in legal and social standing to a consanguineous sibling.[^cite_note-343] Islam has a complex system of rules regarding this, known as *[Rada (fiqh)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rada_\(fiqh\))*. Like the Christian practice of [godparenting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Godparent), milk kinship established a second family that could take responsibility for a child whose biological parents came to harm. "Milk kinship in Islam thus appears to be a culturally distinctive, but by no means unique, institutional form of adoptive kinship."[^cite_note-344]

In [Western](21208200.md) countries, differences in breastfeeding practices have also been observed according to the affiliation or practice of [Christian](5211.md) religions; unaffiliated and [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) women exhibit higher rates of breastfeeding.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348]

### Transgender women

Unsourced or poorly sourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *Find sources:* ["Breastfeeding"](https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia\&q=%22Breastfeeding%22) – [news](https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws\&q=%22Breastfeeding%22+-wikipedia\&tbs=ar\:1) **·** [newspapers](https://www.google.com/search?\&q=%22Breastfeeding%22\&tbs=bkt\:s\&tbm=bks) **·** [books](https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks\:1\&q=%22Breastfeeding%22+-wikipedia) **·** [scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Breastfeeding%22) **·** [JSTOR](https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Breastfeeding%22\&acc=on\&wc=on) *(August 2026)* |
| --- | --- |

Main article: [Breastfeeding by transgender people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breastfeeding_by_transgender_people)

Milk production can be induced in [transgender women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_women) by the use of [hormone medication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminizing_hormone_therapy), usually a [galactagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactagogue) similarly to [induced lactation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Induced_lactation) in [cisgender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cisgender) women, with [domperidone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domperidone) being a common choice.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352] Induced lactation without the use of galactagogues has also been demonstrated in at least two cases using a modified [feminizing hormone therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminizing_hormone_therapy) featuring [progesterone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progesterone) and nipple stimulation, with eventual successful production of breast milk[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354]. In one case report, a transgender woman was able to produce 7 mL of milk per day with hormone treatment.[^cite_note-355] A different transgender woman was able to produce 8 oz of milk per day achieving "sufficient breast milk volume to be the sole source of nourishment for her child for six weeks."[^cite_note-356] Several studies analyzing milk samples from trans women have found them to contain nutrients within parameters suitable for nursing.[^cite_note-357]

A 2018 survey of medical professionals with attending a [WPATH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPATH) event where 34% of the surveyed professionals had met a transgender patient expressing interest in induced lactation, 21% of them knew of providers who facilitated lactation for transgender patients, and 9% had themselves helped trans women induce lactation.[^cite_note-358]

## Workplace



The [International breastfeeding symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_breastfeeding_symbol)

Many mothers have to return to work a short time after their babies have been born.[^cite_note-Abd2012-359] In the U.S., about 70% of mothers with children younger than three years old work full-time, with 1/3 of the mothers returning to work within 3 months and 2/3 returning within 6 months. Working outside of the home and full-time work are significantly associated with lower rates of breastfeeding and breastfeeding for a shorter duration of time.[^cite_note-CDC2018Work-360] According to the [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md), support for breastfeeding in the workplace includes several types of [employee benefits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employee_benefits) and services, including writing corporate policies to support breastfeeding women; teaching employees about breastfeeding; providing designated private space for breastfeeding or expressing milk; allowing [flextime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flextime) to support milk expression during work; giving mothers options for returning to work, such as [remote work](158555.md), [part-time jobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Part-time_job), and extended [maternity leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternity_leave); providing on-site or near-site [child care](389615.md); providing high-quality [breast pumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breast_pump); and offering professional [lactation consultants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactation_consultant).[^cite_note-CDC2018Work-360]

Programs to promote and assist nursing mothers have been found to help maintain breastfeeding.[^cite_note-361] In the United States the CDC reports on a study that "examined the effect of corporate lactation programs on breastfeeding behavior among employed women in California \[which] included prenatal classes, perinatal counseling, and lactation management after the return to work". They found that "about 75% of mothers in the lactation programs continued breastfeeding at least 6 months, although nationally only 10% of mothers employed full-time who initiated breastfeeding were still breastfeeding at 6 months."[^cite_note-CDC2018Work-360]

Section 4207 of the United States' [Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act) (2010) amended the [Fair Labor Standards Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Labor_Standards_Act) and required employers to provide a reasonable break time for an hourly employee to breastfeed a child if the child is less than one year old.[^cite_note-Breastfeedingauto13-362][^cite_note-ppaca-363] The employee must be allowed to breastfeed in a private place, other than a bathroom. The employer is not required to pay the employee during the break time.[^cite_note-ppaca-363] Employers with fewer than 50 employees are not required to comply with the law if doing so would impose an undue hardship to the employer based on its size, finances, nature, or structure of its business.[^cite_note-364]

A 2016 study found that federal policy does not address lactation space functionality and accessibility, policy only protects a subset of employees, and that the enforcement of the policy requires women to file a complaint with the United States Department of Labor. To address each of these issues, the report recommends additional requirements surrounding lactation space and functionality, mandated coverage of exempt employees, and a requirement that employers develop company-specific lactation policies.[^cite_note-Breastfeeding:_The_Illusion_of_Choi-301] As of 2019, the majority of women still did not have access to both accommodations. Working mother advocate and *[Entrepreneur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entrepreneur_\(magazine\))* writer [Christine Michel Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christine_Michel_Carter) documented her experience pumping in a bathroom while working for an employer violating the Fair Labor Standards Act.[^cite_note-365]

In 2022, the Providing Urgent Maternal Protections for Nursing Mothers Act, also known as the PUMP Act, became law in the United States. It mandates that salaried workers who are breastfeeding must have break time and a private space that is not a bathroom. However, employers who do not have 50 or more employees do not have to follow this law if following it would create an undue hardship because of expense or difficulty.[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-Breastfeedingauto13-362]

In April 2018, the U.S. Senate's rules were changed to allow any senator with a child under one year of age to bring them on the Senate floor and breastfeed them during voting.[^cite_note-rules-367]

In [Canada](5042916.md), [British Columbia](3392.md) and [Ontario](22218.md), provincial Human Rights Codes prevent workplace discrimination due to breastfeeding.[^cite_note-infactcanada.ca-368][^cite_note-369] In British Columbia, employers are required to provide accommodation to employees who breastfeed or express breast milk. Although no specific requirements are mandated, under the Human Rights Code, accommodations suggested include paid breaks (not including meal breaks), private facilities that include clean running water, comfortable seating areas, and refrigeration equipment, as well as flexibility in terms of work-related conflicts.[^cite_note-infactcanada.ca-368] In Ontario, employers are encouraged to accommodate breastfeeding employees by providing additional breaks without fear of discrimination. Unlike British Columbia, the Ontario Code does not include specific recommendations and, therefore, leaves significant flexibility for employers.[^cite_note-370]

## Research

Breastfeeding research continues to assess prevalence, HIV transmission, pharmacology, costs, benefits, immunology, contraindications, and comparisons to synthetic breast milk substitutes.[^cite_note-Kramer_2012-6][^cite_note-SahaRyan2015-371] Factors related to the mental health of the nursing mother in the perinatal period have been studied. While cognitive behavior therapy may be the treatment of choice, medications are sometimes used. The use of therapy rather than medication reduces the infant's exposure to medication that may be transmitted through the milk.[^cite_note-MarchesiOssola2016-372] In coordination with institutional organisms, researchers are also studying the social impact of breastfeeding throughout history. Accordingly, strategies have been developed to foster the increase of the breastfeeding rates in the different countries.[^cite_note-373]
